Zest is a Key Ingredient to Good Living
2026-10-17

Zest is a Key Ingredient to Good Living

TIME | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Approaching life with positive expectations, a get-up-and-go attitude, and eagerness is related to many good things in life. This individual asset is often referred to as zest, and it can be purposefully developed to assist us in managing daily stressors as well as creating meaningful lives. True zest is not due to hyperarousal or too much caffeine. It is a cultivated cheerfulness and joy. When we are stressed or not feeling well, it’s hard to avoid doing things halfway or halfheartedly. But there are ways we can increase zest even when it seems like the deck is stacked against us, the world is filled with conflict, and our social media feeds are thick with chaos and gloom. Psychologists Christopher Peterson and Martin Seligman pioneered the field of positive psychology, the scientific study of human flourishing. One of the highlights of their initiatives was published in 2004, Character Strengths and Virtues, a groundbreaking text on the 24 universal character strengths. Zest is one of the 24 strengths. When you cultivate zest, you’ll want to fully engage in life and not just view it from the sidelines, look forward to each day, and wake to anticipation over the day’s possibilities. Zest has also been referred to as vitality, exuberance, and joie de vivre, the French phrase for the joy of living. In an analysis of data from 130 studies involving more than 275,000 people around the globe, most character strengths were associated with greater mental health. But zest was one of just two strengths (the other is hope) that had the strongest relationship to mental well-being across countries and age groups. Zest contributes to life satisfaction among children, adolescents, and adults. In addition, in a study of nearly 10,000 employed adults, those who scored high on zest had higher work satisfaction and were more likely to view work as a calling. People who score high on measures of zest are also more likely to view their lives as meaningful. I reached out to Ryan Niemiec, a psychologist and author of several books, including The Power of Character Strengths and Character Strengths Interventions: A Field Guide for Practitioners. Niemiec is the chief science and education officer at the VIA Institute on Character, a non-profit focused on the science and practice of character strengths. I asked him about the most effective ways people can increase zest in their own lives. “In terms of boosting zest, I turn straight to behavioral activation, one of the most proven strategies for depression,” Niemiec said. “In practical terms, this means to take action in some way to boost oneself—to take that one step forward in lifting one’s energy.” He encouraged people to engage every day in one healthy pleasure, one form of physical activity, and one action that makes you feel like you accomplished something. Some of us seem to naturally have this irresistible aliveness, often invigorating those around us. Others work hard to cultivate zest. Research supports the idea that we are never too old to change. And we can learn how to cultivate zest. We can learn to savor pleasures, set good intentions, and try to view life as an adventure. Other intentional ways to potentially lift our spirits include taking a brisk 10-minute walk every morning, doing cardiovascular or strength-building challenges, practicing Reiki or yoga, laughing with friends, and engaging in stimulating tasks and work. The VIA Institute on Character has activities, workbooks, videos, webinars, and courses—some of them free—on improving all the character strengths, including zest. To be sure, practicing a zest for life can be harder for those who have physical illness and pain and those who engage in unhealthy lifestyle behaviors. And those striving for zest under these circumstances may benefit from assistance from a mental health professional like a psychologist, a physical health provider like a physician, or a personal trainer or fitness instructor. For some of us, being hard on ourselves is second nature. We expect our mental health to stretch under strain. Rather than exclusively focusing on what’s wrong with our mental health, one approach is to develop skills, traits, and perspectives that can help us. Acquiring zest is a worthwhile investment and may help us not only through challenging times, but also help us to see beauty and soar in our lives and workplaces. Once Upon a Time in Harlem Is One of the Great Documentaries of the Year
2026-10-16

Once Upon a Time in Harlem Is One of the Great Documentaries of the Year

TIME | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In 1972, filmmaker William Greaves arranged what we now know was one of the great parties of the 20th century: He commandeered the Harlem apartment of Duke Ellington and arranged for a special group of old friends and colleagues, many of whom had not seen one another in 50 years, to reconnect. The invitees were key figures of the Harlem Renaissance, including muralist Aaron Douglas, poet and novelist Arna Bontemps, society editor Gerri Major, jazz pianist and composer Eubie Blake, and many more. Greaves’ aim was to film the event—he appointed his son, David, as one of the cinematographers—and turn it into a documentary that would serve not just as a record of how these people lived and what they accomplished, but as a way of thinking about how we honor those who are a repository for our past. For a few hours in 1972 Harlem, it was circa 1925 again. This group of people laughing, arguing, and reminiscing about a great era of American history—and also about members of their cohort who were long gone—recaptured lightning in a bottle. And yet the resulting film, Once Upon a Time in Harlem, is one we almost didn’t see. Greaves, who’d made a name for himself with experimental documentaries like Symbiopsychotaxiplasm: Take One (1968), as well as establishing the pioneering public television program Black Journal, considered his Harlem Renaissance film to be one of the most important projects of his life. But although he worked on it off and on for years, he wasn’t able to complete it before his death, in 2014. At last, the film he envisioned has been brought to life by David Greaves and his daughter—William’s granddaughter—producer Liani Greaves. Once Upon a Time in Harlem is one of the great documentaries of its era, and certainly of this year. It’s history that breathes. Ellington's elegantly appointed flat fills up quickly; for the viewer, it’s like dropping into a party right in the middle, as clusters of beautifully dressed people greet one another, embracing, laughing, talking. (Ellington himself was ill and unable to attend.) The guests make themselves at home: Photographer James Van Der Zee, who took extraordinary portraits of the major “show people” of the 1920s, as well as everyday citizens of Harlem, steps up to Ellington’s piano and runs through a few graceful, rippling phrases. We learn that he was trained as a classical musician before turning to photography, just one reminder of the ways people in the arts often need to find a means to support themselves—and sometimes those means can leave us with a lasting record of an era, as we can see from Van Der Zee’s gorgeous, expressive portraits, which flash on the screen like a blessing. Painter and activist Richard Price Nugent, when asked what it was like to be a part of the Renaissance, can’t really say. “While you’re living it, it is the now,” he reflects. Actor Leigh Whipper, the first Black man to join Actors Equity and one of the oldest in the group, and publisher and bookseller Richard B. Moore, recite long poems and passages from the era by memory. Moore calls up Claude McKay’s brilliant, lacerating poem “If We Must Die” as if he’d just learned it yesterday, but you know he’s been living with it for years. Conversations swell through the room in waves, often prompted by Greaves himself, who was not just a filmmaker but also a poet and sculptor, a man who acted and danced. (The film has been brilliantly edited by Anne DeMare and Lynn True.) Ida Mae Cullen, the peppery widow of poet Countee Cullen, pipes up to remind the group, vigorously, that the Harlem Renaissance really lasted for just one decade, the 1920s, though its ripples began years before and resonated for decades after. For this group, politics and art are so entwined as to be indistinguishable. At one point, a vigorous yet civil cross-talking argument breaks out over the principles and philosophy of Back-to-Africa activist Marcus Garvey: there’s mutual respect in this room, but no such thing as consensus. Among other things, Once Upon a Time in Harlem might make you yearn for an era when people, including intellectuals, really knew how to dress: society editor Major, resplendent in a bold, patterned dress, sports about 10 glittery bracelets on each wrist and pulls it off with aplomb. Aaron Douglas, Jean Blackwell Hutson, Richard Bruce Nugent, Eubie Blake, and Irvin C. Miller Courtesy of Neon What’s striking about Once Upon a Time in Harlem is the seemingly casual quality most of these captured conversations. But casual isn’t the same as insignificant. Regina Andrews, a regal woman decked out in a white lace dress and vivid turquoise earrings and necklace, speaks of the job she started as a young woman, as a librarian at the 135th Street branch of the New York Public Library. Shortly after she was hired, her superiors explained that certain poets and authors would often come to the library to work; they had special rooms reserved for them. She and her roommate became friendly with many of these luminaries, among them Cullen and Langston Hughes, and would often host little get-togethers in their apartment. The problem? They didn’t have enough dishes to go around. And so one New Year’s Day, when Andrews and her friends set out on a tour of the area’s Chinese restaurants, the men—unbeknownst to her—collected a few plates here and a few cups there. Suddenly, Andrews had enough dishes and cutlery for everybody, pieces she treasured for years. Maybe that sounds like a minor thing, having enough dishes to entertain your friend group of poets and writers. But as Andrews tells that story, it feels anything but small. “It was just the joy of living and creating together,” she explains—as good a summation as any of what it must have been like to be alive in such a glorious time, a “now” that, thanks to Greaves and his family, becomes a part of our lives too.

Daisy Edgar-Jones Is the Austen Heroine We Need in a Charming Sense and Sensibility
2026-10-16

Daisy Edgar-Jones Is the Austen Heroine We Need in a Charming Sense and Sensibility

TIME | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings When news got around that a new movie version of Sense and Sensibility was in the works, cries of dismay rang forth from Bath to Brooklyn. How could anyone dare try to top Ang Lee’s 1995 adaptation, penned by—and starring, alongside Kate Winslet— Emma Thompson? But the idea that fine adaptations of great books need to be stopping points, rather than springboards, is alarming by itself. Why is a new adaptation of any Jane Austen novel such a bad thing? There are always new movie audiences, eager for their own fresh take. And just as important, why shouldn’t another generation of actors have a crack at these roles? Our ability to stream anything we want, whenever we want, hasn’t necessarily made us more adventurous; sometimes it’s just a way of clinging to familiar favorites at the expense of welcoming anything new. Austen herself, with her aversion to sentimentality, would have disapproved. Then what would she make of Georgia Oakley’s amiable, if occasionally a little sluggish, reading of her debut novel, published anonymously in 1811? It’s hard to say for sure, but there’s nothing that’s offensive about this new Sense and Sensibility, and plenty that’s lovely. Daisy Edgar-Jones and Esmé Creed-Miles play Elinor and Marianne Dashwood, sisters in a close-knit family whose temperamental differences temporarily drive them apart. As the film opens, Elinor and Marianne, along with their mother (Caitríona Balfe) and younger sister Margaret (Bodhi Rae Breathnach), are just learning that they’ll soon have to vacate the airy, comfortable house they’ve called home for years. The girls’ father has just died; his property will not go to his daughters, but to his son from a previous marriage, John (Tom Brooke), who nevertheless hopes to do right by making sure his father's second family well provided for. But his disagreeable wife Fanny (Stacy Martin), feels differently, and to maintain their pride, the Dashwood women choose to move into far more modest quarters, in an arrangement brokered by a family friend, Mrs. Jennings (a blissfully dithery Fiona Shaw). Mrs. Jennings is also an unrepentant meddler, and she believes she can easily marry off the two eldest Dashwood girls. Ever-sensible Elinor, who never asks for much, has already met, and fallen for, Edward Ferrars (George MacKay), Fanny’s brother, as kind and modest as Fanny is arrogant. And Marianne, a dreamer who lives for music and books, has her sights set on flirty, fun Mr. Willoughby (Frank Dillane): He loves to laugh and sing and dance, and Marianne is sure he’s perfect for her, though another potential suitor, the stalwart and rather dull Colonel Brandon (Herbert Nordrum), is also circling. If you’ve never read Austen’s lively and perceptive novel—or seen either Lee’s 1995 version or the 2008 BBC miniseries adaptation—you’re left to guess which men the sisters will end up with, which is just one of the story’s delights. Daisy Edgar-Jones and Esmé Creed-Miles Courtesy of Focus Features But as always with Austen, there’s more to love than romance: The economics of “making a good match” are always, by necessity, lurking somewhere in these characters’ minds. In the case of the sisters Marianne and Elinor—one too dreamy, the other perhaps not dreamy enough—the differences in what they look for in a partner are almost enough to pull them apart. Oakley, who previously directed the 2022 drama Blue Jean, has a deft touch with the material and a deep respect for the English countryside setting. There are drowsy closeups of wildflowers, and shots of placidly grazing deer; a country dance feels lively and lived-in, contrasted with a later ball, set in London, where the steps are more formal and regimented—Oakley leaves no doubt as to which is more fun. Shot by Arnaud Potier, the film has a handsome, genteel look. And if at times the movie comes off as a bit decorous and stiff, the actors, particularly Edgar-Jones and Creed-Miles, do their best to keep the proceedings whirring. Creed-Miles shows that there’s a slightly self-centered quality to Marianne’s romantic preoccupations—she takes pride in wearing her heart on her sleeve, though in some sense it’s also a means of getting her way. And yet as Creed-Miles plays her, it’s impossible to dislike her. When Marianne become truly hurt, we hurt for her, thanks to the bruised anguish in Creed-Miles’ eyes. And Edgar-Jones plays Elinor, the practical sister, with just the right amount of subdued fire. Elinor is the one who always matches her expectations with reality, and as much as she loves Edward Ferrars, she doesn’t put up much of a fight when she learns she might have to give him up. Edgar-Jones gives us clues to Elinor’s submerged longing, but only in flashes. And in a pivotal scene, where Marianne accuses Elinor, angrily, of “always being right,” we can see how wounded she is, even as she recognizes that Marianne is onto something: Elinor would rather be correct than look foolish. Edgar-Jones plays Elinor as reserved without making her recessive; we can see the ways in which she wants to want more. In an age of lowered expectations—for movies, for romance, for just about everything—maybe she’s the Austen heroine we need, ready to break out of the box she’s drawn around herself. Austen knew what she was talking about, and time hasn’t eroded her wisdom.

Carney Warns U.S. Is Trying to ‘Break’ Canada as Trump Revives ‘51st State’ Threat
2026-10-16

Carney Warns U.S. Is Trying to ‘Break’ Canada as Trump Revives ‘51st State’ Threat

TIME | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings U.S. President Donald Trump and Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney at the G7 summit, in Evian, France, on June 16, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney on Thursday warned that the United States is trying to sow discord as he urged Albertans not to vote for a separation referendum. “The stakes are high. This referendum, it comes at the worst possible time. The world has become more dangerous and divided,” Carney said at a rally in Calgary ahead of Monday’s vote, which will see residents decide whether the oil-rich province should hold a referendum on independence. “There are some outside our borders who are working to break us, so they can own us.” While Carney didn’t explicitly name the U.S., the remarks echoed his previous warnings of the dangers of American dominance. Carney's statement came a day after U.S. President Donald Trump revisited his ambitions to annex Canada, telling the crowd at a rally in Arizona that the country will “end up being the 51st state, because it's the only way it works.” Trump again accused Canada of treating the U.S. “very badly” and vowed he won’t allow it to happen anymore as “it’s a whole different ballgame now [that] Trump is in town.” The U.S. and Canada remain in a contentious trade standoff after negotiations collapsed in August. Canada’s retaliatory tariffs came into effect last month, matching the economic pressure imposed by the U.S. Carney has encouraged Canadians to band together and take back their economic power by choosing Canadian products over those made in the U.S. The trade tussle comes after months of Trump repeatedly threatening Canada’s sovereignty—a move that has stoked tensions between the U.S. Administration and Canadian officials. In light of Trump’s most recent remarks about possible annexation, Canada’s former deputy Prime Minister Chrystia Freeland said it would be dangerous to deepen or maintain close economic ties with a neighbor that “openly says they want to take you over.” Carney admitted in an interview with the New York Times last month that he had studied the risk of a U.S. invasion, given Trump’s charged rhetoric. “I think you have a responsibility in these roles to look at extreme tail risk,” he said. “That’s just risk management. That’s not a base case, but it would be irresponsible not to [prepare].” Amid the U.S.-Canada tensions, Carney is desperately trying to promote his “Canada Strong” strategy—one that he hopes will very much include Alberta. Polling conducted by 338Canada suggests that as of Oct. 11, 75% of Albertans would vote to remain part of Canada. But Carney’s resolve is being tested on more than one front, after the separatist Parti Québécois won Quebec's provincial election earlier this month. Incoming Premier Paul St-Pierre Plamondon has already stated his intention to hold a referendum on Quebec independence. While he has vowed not to initiate a vote so long as Trump is in office, the looming presence of a separatist movement undoubtedly complicates matters for Carney at a time when he is striving to show that Canada is strong enough to prosper without relying on the American market. With the U.S. and Canada locked in a trade stalemate, and with annexation threats once again dominating the conversation, here’s a look at the reality Carney is facing as he deals with both domestic and international pressures. Trump’s accusations that Canada has taken advantage of the U.S. on matters of trade and security date back to before Carney became Prime Minister in March 2025, meaning the friction we’re currently seeing goes deeper than a simple clash between Trump and Carney. Trump would often refer to Carney's predecessor Justin Trudeau as the "governor" of the "great state of Canada." In terms of any physical threat facing Canada, Patrick Leblond, a professor of public and international affairs at the University of Ottawa, says that Carney “put it best when he talked about tail risk” in his interview with the Times. “It’s the financial term for something that has potentially high impact, but low probability,” Leblond explains. Trump’s economic actions and threats have proven to be far more damaging than his annexation remarks, experts note. “He has weaponized market access to the U.S.,” says Leblond. “What we can expect is more economic pressure from the White House in the coming months. Trump and his acolytes certainly [appear to] see Canada as a small, weak country that the United States should try to squeeze further.” The U.S. is Canada’s largest trading partner, with almost $2.6 billion worth of goods and services passing across the border daily in 2024. According to the Office for the U.S. Trade Representative, trade between the two was down 4.6% from 2024 to 2025, but still saw $872.3 billion worth of goods and services cross the border. Still, there are concerns that any suggestion of annexation, however unlikely it may be, could damage Carney’s high-stakes domestic campaign to unite the country. On the home front, the success of a separatist party in Quebec is another threat to the unity that Carney is striving for. However, despite the Parti Québécois' victory (they fell five seats short of securing a majority government), the desire for separatism among Quebec residents remains low—with only 31% in favor of sovereignty for the province, according to data from 338 Canada released on Oct. 11. “If Quebec is on a path to separation or not, it would never be because they were looking for further alignment with the United States,” argues Lori Turnbull, a professor in political science at Dalhousie University. “Quebec has a variety of cultural protections in the Canadian federation. They would have none of that in the United States.” The more immediate risk for Carney lies in Alberta, with residents preparing to vote on whether the province should hold a referendum on independence. Carney, who was raised in Edmonton, Alberta, on Thursday framed the referendum as an opportunity for voters to “stand up for Alberta, the heart of Canada” and “recognize that we're all responsible for each other.” Ahead of the vote, residents express their views with signs outside their homes in Edmonton, Alberta, on Sep. 28, 2026. John Diep—Getty Images The pressure and rhetoric from the U.S. could actually work in Carney’s favor, experts tell TIME. “The effect of Trump's comments has been unifying,” says Turnbull. Despite discussions of independence in Alberta and Quebec, she says “there isn't widespread panic that the country is coming apart.” In fact, research conducted by the Environics Institute for Survey Research in 2025 found that “in almost every province, the proportion of residents who are very attached to Canada is greater than the proportion who are very attached to their province.” Between Trudeau’s resignation on Jan. 6, 2025, and a federal election in Canada months later on April 28, Carney’s Liberal Party went from 20% to 42.8% in the polls, according to CBC, with Carney’s message of defiance in the face of Trump proving to be a rallying point. This falls in line with depleting favorable views of the U.S. among Canadians. In July, 44% of Canadians had a favorable opinion of China compared to 33% of Canadians who held the same view of the U.S., according to a Pew Research Center survey. “When China is more popular than the U.S. in a country like Canada, you have to ask yourself: ‘What's going on?’” says Leblond. Continued annexation threats could bring Canadians even closer together.  “Already, trust is very low, and [more threats] would confirm even more that the United States is [perhaps] not a trustworthy partner at this time,” Leblond argues. Since the U.S.-Canada trade war reignited in August, Carney has seen his domestic approval rating bounce back up. On Oct. 15, it stood at 62% according to Poliwave, compared to 56% in mid-July. Carney’s resistance against American economic pressures could help his cause, particularly in possible separatist provinces. “In August, when Carney walked out of the negotiations with the U.S., the highest levels of support for him were found in Quebec,” says Leblond. Indeed, Carney’s approval rating in Quebec grew from 76% to 80% between Aug. 17, and Sep. 7, the highest in any province, according to Liaison Strategies. Turnbull says the support behind Carney’s decision to walk away from negotiations prompted a strong sense of “patriotism and loyalty.” That attitude among Canadians has been reflected in the burgeoning “Buy Canadian” movement, in which consumers across the country have joined a boycott of U.S. products in an effort to feed back into the Canadian economy. In September, European Commission president Ursula von der Leyen invited Canada to work toward becoming the bloc’s first “associate member,” in what she described as an “urgent” reimagining of partnerships. While addressing the European Parliament as a guest of honor in Strasbourg, France, Carney insisted “Europe and Canada are stronger together” and said “Canada and the European Union can anchor our ground to hold our values while forming a protective canopy under which our citizens can thrive.” Trump called the unique membership proposal “laughable” and has threatened economic punishments if the E.U. moves forward with von der Leyen’s plan. Carney’s shift towards other partnerships “doesn't mean that Canada is not going to do business with the U.S., but it's no longer a preferred partner,” says Leblond, noting that Carney wants to build relations with partners that “share the same values in terms of rules-based trade and investment.” In January, after meeting with Chinese President Xi Jinping, Carney announced a new strategic partnership with China, focused on “energy, agri-food, and trade.” Carney agreed to cut his country’s 100% tariff on Chinese electric cars in return for lower tariffs on Canadian farm products, including canola seeds, a major Canadian export. These stronger international ties that diversify trade and security away from the U.S. are proving popular on the homefront, according to data. According to Abacus Data, a survey conducted in September found that 49% of Canadians would support Canada joining the E.U. An Ipsos poll conducted in January, in light of Carney’s visit to China, found that 54% of Canadians also said they support stronger trade ties with China.

The sizes are already out of this world and trying to really comprehend the enormous size differences between celestial bodies is likely beyond our imagination. Sure, we can "know" the sizes with numbers but to really "feel" the enormity is unlikely. Our world feels large as an individual on the surface. Jupiter is nearly 11x larger in radius then the Earth and the Sun is roughly 10x larger then Jupiter. T We are amoeba trying to comprehend an elephant. I mean, normally green stars aren't real, to the human eye. The problem is that from a standard blackbody curve [libretexts.org], as you go up in surface temperature, by the time you've flipped the red-green cone activation ratio to the green side, you're getting so much blue that it looks like white instead. And then if you keep going, blue dominates and it turns blue. So it goes red, yellow, white, blue, not stopping for green. You also don't get purple because, for the most part, purple needs a strong red activation, little green, alongside at least a reasonable amount of blue - but with stars, the blue is way stronger than the red under a typical blackbody curve (there actually is a small shortwave red activation peak, so you *can* get purple purely with shortwave (like in a rainbow), but in the blackbody spectrum, the blue is so dominant that its not enough). The trick here is that specific emission and absorption spectra can deviate from a standard blackbody curve. It may seem weird because one is used to sodium emission lamps being orange. But in the star's ~4000K photosphere, pressure broadening smears out the sodium doublet into a massive absorption trough, absorbing red to yellow light. Accreted calcium also has a similar effect on the blue side of the spectrum. Look back at the 4000K blackbody curve in the above link, imagine even a relatively minor suppression in both red and blue, and you can see how it morphs into a curve that the eye will read as green. :) "It is green" Scotty, ST"It is..hm.. green" Data, STNG "Are we green?" - Korben Dallas"(choking) Supergreen" - Ruby Rhod It is just addition of other wavelengths and subtraction of blue makes it seemingly yellow... The Supreme Court’s Right Flank Wants to Give You Fewer Privacy Rights Than a Famous Judge
2026-10-17

The Supreme Court’s Right Flank Wants to Give You Fewer Privacy Rights Than a Famous Judge

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. In 2022, college sports fan Michael Salazar became a digital subscriber to 247Sports.com, a website that shares news about NCAA football and basketball. In order to register for the website, owned by Paramount Global, Salazar signed up for 247Sports’ online newsletter. And he used his subscription to view sports-related video media. Quietly embedded within those videos, though, was a tracking pixel created by Facebook. Because Paramount installed Facebook’s tracking pixel on the 247Sports website, every time Salazar requested or viewed a video, Paramount automatically disclosed the name and URL of the video to Facebook, along with Salazar’s Facebook ID, the unique identifier Facebook assigns to all of its users. Facebook then used that information to show Salazar targeted advertisements. Privacy violations like this have become a routine part of life on the internet. According to a 2024 study on website privacy, more than half of all companies listed in the S&P 500 index use the “Meta pixel” on their sites, sharing people’s information with both Facebook and Instagram. But in theory, there are at least some legal boundaries. Back in 1988, after a D.C. journalist obtained and published a list of movies rented by Supreme Court nominee Robert Bork, a scandalized Congress passed the Video Privacy Protection Act. To be clear: Congress was scandalized by the nonconsensual disclosure of Bork’s rental history, not the judge’s viewing habits, which were pretty unremarkable (unlike those of another judicial nominee who would be confirmed to the Supreme Court a few years later.) The VPPA prohibits videotape service providers from knowingly disclosing the personally identifiable information of consumers, unless they get the consumers’ written consent. The statute defines “videotape service provider” to include any person in the business of delivering “prerecorded video cassette tapes or similar audio visual materials.” And the definition of “consumer” includes any “subscriber of goods or services” from the provider. Here, Salazar subscribed to Paramount’s sports website. He requested audiovisual materials from Paramount, and Paramount delivered them. And then Paramount knowingly disclosed what he watched to Facebook without getting his consent. In September 2022, Salazar filed a class-action lawsuit, arguing that Paramount violated the VPPA. But in July 2023, the federal district court dismissed his complaint, reasoning that Salazar didn’t count as a “consumer” under the statute because he was only subscribed to a newsletter, “not audio visual content.” Salazar appealed to the 6th U.S. Circuit Court of Appeals, but in April 2025, a divided three-judge panel affirmed the dismissal, agreeing with the district court that Salazar’s newsletter subscription didn’t make him a subscriber under the law. Then Salazar appealed to the Supreme Court, which heard oral arguments in Salazar v. Paramount on Wednesday. Under the plain text of the Video Privacy Protection Act, Salazar should be able to hold Paramount liable for its disclosure of his video history. But during the oral arguments, the Republican-nominated justices peppered Salazar’s attorney with questions suggesting that imposing an atextual limitation onto the statute—one that would conveniently insulate corporations from legal liability when they intrude on consumers’ privacy for profit—was really just the commonsense thing to do. “Wouldn’t it be natural to think that goods or services from a videotape service provider would be video-related services?” asked Justice Clarence Thomas. Later in the oral argument, Thomas said he “always thought of 247Sports as a sports site,” and he questioned the “logic” of viewing the site as a “videotape service provider.” Justice Samuel Alito further argued that the law was “based on the technology of the 1980s,” when people had to “go to a Blockbuster store and get a VHS tape.” Although the text of the VPPA appears to cover Salazar’s claims, Alito suggested that a newsletter subscription isn’t analogous enough to what Congress had in mind and asked Salazar’s counsel why he thought the “smart lower court judges” who ruled against him rejected his “very literal interpretation” of the statute. Justice Brett Kavanaugh echoed these concerns: “We are a textualist Court but usually not a literalist Court,” he said. Kavanaugh continued, “Congress in the 1980s wasn’t thinking about data sharing for targeted advertising.” Kavanaugh and Justice Amy Coney Barrett also worried that, if the VPPA weren’t limited to the long-defunct Blockbusters of the world, practically any business could find themselves liable under the law. Kavanaugh brought up concerns, raised by corporate interest groups in amicus briefs, that “every business in America that has video on its website” could be targeted. Trying to suss out the scope of the statute, Kavanaugh and Barrett both asked how long a video must be for a company to count as a video service provider. Salazar’s attorney indicated that length wasn’t especially relevant and shifted the conversation to the business and profit motive of delivering videos, but Barrett was skeptical. “If I’m on Nordstrom’s website and I click to see the model show me what the shoes are going to look like,” said Barrett, “that’s a profit motive.” For all the Republican justices’ stated concern on Wednesday about reading and interpreting the VPPA in a way that makes sense, they failed to meaningfully engage with the text and context. Paramount provided Salazar with videos and then told Facebook what videos he watched without his consent—the exact kind of injury contemplated by the statute. Technology and commerce have obviously changed considerably since Congress passed the Video Privacy Protection Act. But the law’s commitment to protecting the privacy of video consumers has not. What has changed, however, is the ideological commitments of the people who sit on the court. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. You’ve heard that grit matters. But is grit alone the secret to success?
2026-10-17

You’ve heard that grit matters. But is grit alone the secret to success?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
In Situated: Find the People and Places That Bring Out Your Best, Angela Duckworth explores how your situation—the objects around you, your peers, your mentors, your physical environment—shapes who you become. In this excerpt, she explains her central framework: the three boxes model of human behavior (situation, thoughts, response) and how understanding this can help you reverse-engineer your own behavior and make intentional changes to your circumstances. This partial transcript has been edited and condensed for clarity. John Dickerson:Now that you’ve been on a book tour for a while, what is your simplest way of helping people understand? I mean, willpower is a great word by the way, right? The “power of the will.” The science of willpower is that there is part of the brain toward the front, toward the lateral prefrontal cortex, that is in charge of ordering around the rest of your brain. And you can force yourself to do things that you don’t want to do in the moment. You can say, “I don’t really want to look at this Google Doc, and pound through another revision of this memo that I’m writing,” but you can force yourself to do it. You can force yourself to stop eating— “I’m just going to put that french fry down.” That’s your dorsolateral prefrontal cortex at work. We all know what it feels like, even children recognize the feeling of effort, the effort of suppressing impulses. By the way, I need to tell you, John, that’s not grit.Willpower is not grit. People do not become Olympic gold medalists because they suppress impulses all day long. They have a different motivation. But let’s talk about willpower because so many of us understand that that has been part of our success, that we have done that before. We have forced ourselves not to eat things, to eat things, to drink things, not to drink things, to wake up, to get along… But here’s what the science of willpower has said over the last 20 years: When scientists began to study very successful people in terms of people who say, “This is my goal for the week,” you come back to them, and you’re like, “Did you achieve the goal?” Now, we can understand the people who did achieve their goal, versus those who did not. What was revealed was quite surprising, which is that it’s actually not willpower that’s doing most of the work. People who achieve their goals avoid the distractions and temptations that get in the way. They avoid the friction. They actually create opportunities where it is the natural and automatic thing to eat the way they want, drink the way they want, exercise the way they want, work the way they want, even relate to other people the way they want. And so, that actually is what’s been occupying me in large part for the last 10+ years in my research. And I think that was one reason I felt like I needed to complete the story, not because grit and willpower are the same thing, but of course we have goals that come into conflict with our immediate desires—gritty people too—but it was clear to me from the research that I was doing, and the other scientists who work on behavior change, that this American myth of willpower is so dangerously incomplete. And part of that myth is the conflation between willpower and grit. So, I’ve demonstrated something for listeners- Well, we got to nuance, John, which I think is only possible in a real conversation. It Was Elon Musk All Along
2026-10-17

It Was Elon Musk All Along

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
The Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, a politics newsletter that, since its redesign, is looking sharper than Jon Husted’s ass. We have only two-and-a-half more weeks until these damnable midterms are over, after which all politics will cease and this will become a newsletter about spelunking. Hooray! This week: Donald Trump is so mad about fuel prices screwing up the midterms that he might just give Europe to Russia. A White House press secretary has come and gone since we last wrote, and now she can return to being a lawyer (maybe?). Jon Ossoff said WHAT? There are competitive Senate races WHERE? But first! It was super PAC donor disclosure week, and you’ll never guess who’s spending the most money this election cycle. (Hint: It’s the guy with the most money.) Recall in the spring, during the Texas primary season, the big conundrum that Senate Republicans faced over money. Their concern was that if baggage-laden Attorney General Ken Paxton defeated Sen. John Cornyn for the nomination, the party would have to spend hundreds of millions of dollars to (attempt to) rescue him in the fall, money that it could be spending in traditional battlegrounds. Then, after Paxton rode Trump’s last-minute endorsement to win the primary, there was a lengthy standoff between irritated Senate Republicans and Trump’s super PAC over who should foot the bill to rescue Paxton. The conundrum was resolved around Labor Day. Trump’s MAGA Inc. has put $30 million into the state. But it’s Texas PAC, a spinoff of Senate Republicans’ Senate Leadership Fund, that’s done the bulk of the work, having poured $148 million into the state as of this writing. It both did and didn’t come as a surprise that Senate Republicans so easily let themselves be pushed around by Trump’s operation into cleaning up Trump’s mess. But with third-quarter fundraising filings now coming in, we can see the deal that was struck: Senate Republicans’ PAC spent the rescue money, but they were just a vessel. The guy really picking up the tab was Elon Musk. Senate Leadership Fund announced this week that it raised $273 million in the third quarter, with a full $158 million of that—58 percent—coming from Musk. In total, Musk has now given a jokelike $366 million this cycle, making him comfortably the biggest donor of either party. With that $158 million gift to the Senate Leadership Fund, he made months of Republican worries about how to (attempt to!) rescue Paxton look foolish. It was resolved with two checks, two strokes of the pen. No hard decisions over resources would have to be made after all. For all the attention Musk got during the 2024 campaign and the early part of Trump’s second term, it’s gone relatively unnoticed the staggering degree to which he’s shaping these midterms with his checkbook. That’s not by accident. He’s learned that people want his money but not him. From the 2024 election through the first few months of 2025, Musk was everywhere: On the campaign trail prancing about onstage with Trump, living at Mar-a-Lago, attending inauguration and inauguration rallies, shredding the federal government apart, fishing around the Treasury Department’s payment system, wielding chain saws at conservative conferences, and trying to win Wisconsin Supreme Court elections. Musk eventually crashed out and receded back to his companies (which were suffering in the stock market during his extracurricular activities) and said he would spend “a lot less” on politics going forward. “I think I’ve done enough,” he said in May 2025. (Over that summer, he and Trump would have a spectacular falling out over social media. They’ve since repaired things.) Musk, it turns out, has not spent “a lot less” on politics—if we’re talking dollars. He has now spent more on the midterms than he spent to help elect Donald Trump in 2024. He’s just done it more quietly, without attaching the force of his weird personality as a condition. He is not skipping around onstage behind Ken Paxton in Texas. He’s just funding his candidacy. In full. He has taken the most expensive race of the cycle off of Trump, the Senate GOP’s, and the candidate’s plate. We know it’s ho-hum to observe that there’s too much money in campaigns. But this is really something! Musk has reshaped the midterms with an amount of money that comfortably cruises past any other donor’s contributions this cycle but is effectively less than couch change to him—an amount of money he’s made in the time it’s taken us to write this (run-on, sure) sentence. Is that how our elections should work? Guess it depends on who you ask. For a while now, the Trump administration has been blaming the spike in diesel prices on Ukrainian attacks on Russian refineries. He’s not wrong that it’s a meaningful factor. But it’s an exacerbating factor atop the underlying cause of the Iran war. Trump can’t blame spiking prices on the Iran war, of course, since he was the one who done did it. Laying blame at the feet of the Ukrainians, whom MAGA has been unusually hostile to for years, serves the White House’s political interest heading into the midterms. And man, does that political interest suck for geopolitics. To spite Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky, Trump reached a diesel deal with Russia—just weeks after signing into law a bill allowing the president to tariff the top importers of Russian energy in the hopes of cutting off funding for the Russian war machine. (Trump has yet to announce any self-tariffs.) That indeed did tick off Zelensky, who then ticked off Trump, who said Ukraine needs a new president. Things have only gotten worse from there. The Ukrainians this week reported a disruption in their access to Starshield, the military-grade satellite network that Ukraine uses for battlefield communications and drone operation—and Reuters reported that the order to do this came from the White House. Zelensky appears to be finished pretending to be nice to Trump and has resumed attacks on Russian energy infrastructure against White House wishes. Say what you will about the guy, but Lindsey Graham’s back-channeling between the two is conspicuously absent right now. The worse the midterms go for Republicans, the wider the universe of blame Trump will cast. The only thing that’s certain—like, certain—is that Trump will not question whether he, by making extremely optional policy choices that raised the cost of living, contributed to the losses. No, the blame will be cast fully outward, from the inner rings of staff to political consultants, to candidates, to RINOs, to perceived Democratic cheaters. And it will be cast globally, to figures doing what is necessary to save their country from invasion. As a B-plot for the October of an election year, we might as well have a White House press secretary get hired and fired in the same week. So it went with Katie Zacharia, a member of the broader MAGA extended universe whose two or three public appearances in her new role were bad enough for Trump to ax her on Friday. This was all on top of questions about whether she was actually an attorney, a simple question that she had a difficult time answering. Who’s up next? Who cares? A lot less attention should be paid to who holds this position and whether they’re “good” at it. Being “good” at White House press secretary means you’re able to not get flustered on live television while spinning terrible news. Assessing them is merely judging a lying contest. Joe Biden’s last press secretary, Karine Jean-Pierre, got big-time flustered trying to pretend that Biden was not deep into senescence—low marks. Boo! Zacharia’s predecessor, Karoline Leavitt, did not get flustered pretending that Trump was doing a good and coherent job as president—flying colors. Zacharia, meanwhile, received the equivalent of a “see me after class” from the teacher in lieu of a grade. It’s just a parlor game. • The race in which we’re noticing a modest increase in Democratic alarm is Michigan’s Senate contest. Democrats did, after all, nominate a left-wing candidate in a purple state, and even in this environment, it wasn’t going to be easy getting him across the line. Where the worry has materialized is over his support with Black voters, among whom Abdul El-Sayed performed poorly in the primary. Our colleague Aymann Ismail does an excellent job explaining the nature of the concerns here. And both El-Sayed and Mike Rogers’ campaign decisions show that this softness in the El-Sayed coalition is blinking on their radars. El-Sayed has already campaigned with Kamala Harris in Detroit, and next Saturday, he’ll be campaigning with Barack Obama in Detroit. That’s heavy weaponry for turning out Black voters. And the Rogers campaign is paying more attention to Black voters in Detroit than Republicans typically do. It’s not that Rogers needs big inroads among Black voters. But he does need to maintain that softness for El-Sayed. • Back to the Texas Senate race: You know, money isn’t everything, although it’s definitely great. But is it serving its intended purpose of saving Ken Paxton? Well, a Truth Social post from Trump about how if Paxton loses, it will all be John Cornyn and Karl Rove’s fault doesn’t indicate that GOP internal polling has Paxton in a great position. (James Talarico isn’t broke, by the way. As it happens, he smashed the single-quarter Senate candidate fundraising record with a reported $64 million haul in the third quarter.) • How seriously should we take Georgia Sen. Jon Ossoff’s pledge from his debate this week? Asked whether he would commit to serving his full six-year term should he win reelection, he responded: “Yes, I will. I have zero interest in running for president. I’m not running for president.” This will come as a disappointment to the many fans that Sen. McDreamboat has picked up as he (probably!) cruises to reelection in a swing state. So can he get out of it? Sure. If he loses the presidential race in either the primary or the general, he will serve his full six-year Senate term, just like he said. And if he wins, then who cares whether those Georgia voters felt lied to—he’s gonna be president! But it’s also worth observing: He may be completely honest about how he has zero interest in running for president next year. Running for president sucks. • You know how last week we wrote about (a) how Senate control could all come down to Maine and how (b) debates don’t really matter, until the moment they do? It probably would have been best for Troy Jackson, who’s been criticized for hotheaded episodes involving women in the past, to make it through his fourth and final debate against Sen. Susan Collins without telling her to “shut up.” • Ossoff or No-ssoff, it’s still too early to tell who’ll have a leg up in the 2028 Democratic presidential primary, with dozens of candidates seemingly inter—ah, never mind. Indiana county council candidate Jaime West has the nomination locked up. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. If you value our work, please disable your ad blocker. Ilhan Omar’s D.C. home—apparently targeting immigration statements made when she was 12 years old, by a father who is now deceased—all in service of a reported plan to strip her of her citizenship and deport her. Trump Is So Desperate to Lower Gas Prices That He’s Throwing a Key U.S. Ally Under the Bus
2026-10-16

Trump Is So Desperate to Lower Gas Prices That He’s Throwing a Key U.S. Ally Under the Bus

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
This story is adapted from the Slatest newsletter. You can sign up here to get it in your inbox every day. It’s been a rough week for Ukraine, less because of anything happening on the battlefield and more because Donald Trump’s hot-and-cold relationship with the embattled country has once again gone frigid. Last Friday, Trump announced a plan to import millions of barrels of Russian diesel, which has been under U.S. sanctions since shortly after Russia started the war with Ukraine. Trump savaged his Ukrainian counterpart, Volodymyr Zelensky, over the weekend, saying, “I think it’s time for Ukraine to get a new president.” And this week, according to Reuters, the White House temporarily limited Ukraine’s access to a SpaceX-made satellite system it uses for battlefield communications. Those developments have understandably sent people who care about Ukraine’s future spiraling, convinced that the ostensible leader of the free world has shown his true cards. Buying Russian diesel would effectively subsidize Vladimir Putin’s war effort (even as U.S. taxpayers are already helping arm Ukraine). Ukraine’s satellite access seems to be coming back, but the interruption foiled attacks and cost Ukraine military drones. Zelensky appears to be losing patience, too, expressing betrayal after Trump demanded Ukraine stop attacking Russian energy infrastructure even as Russia continued to attack Ukraine’s. Seen in the context of Trump’s long-standing admiration for Putin, it’s hard to conclude that these developments are anything but bad for Ukraine. But is Trump’s turnabout permanent? At the risk of sounding like a contrarian, the truth is that he has waffled over the war more often than not. In some moments, like when he accused Zelensky of being insufficiently grateful for U.S. support last year, Trump has taken Putin’s side. But begrudgingly or not, Trump has also meaningfully helped Ukraine by agreeing to let it manufacture American weapon systems and by joining Europe in levying more sanctions against Russia over the summer. One reason Trump’s latest pivot against Ukraine might not last is that his diesel deal looks more like an act of desperation than a lasting strategic shift. As we’ve explained before, Trump is scrambling to lower U.S. gas prices, which his war of choice in Iran has elevated, ahead of the midterm elections. Despite his claim that prices “will be COMING DOWN, IN RECORD NUMBERS, AND FAST!,” the deal probably won’t do much to alleviate Americans’ pain at the pump before Election Day. But getting caught trying rarely hurts, and even small decreases could be enough to save endangered Republican candidates, who can use all the help they can get. Trump approaches international relations in personalist terms (he often describes foreign leaders as his friends, even when they aren’t), so his pivot back to bashing Zelensky is arguably a more worrying development. But a second reason to doubt whether it will last is that a surprising number of Republicans are pushing back. Several GOP members of Congress have joined Democrats in objecting to the diesel deal. Republicans facing tough races—including Ashley Hinson in Iowa and Mike Rogers in Michigan—have also criticized it. Republicans don’t stand up to Trump often, but their pushback over Ukraine has helped block some of his more damaging efforts, including trying to withhold U.S. weapons in his first term and threatening to pull the U.S. out of NATO in his second. None of that makes Trump’s turnabout, however long it lasts, any less of a betrayal. Whether his affinity for Putin owes to political desperation, autocratic envy, or profit motive (Trump’s negotiations to end the war reportedly include an oil deal that would enrich investors in his family’s ventures), it has already cost Ukrainian lives. But it may also come back to haunt Trump. By placing the U.S. in the position of supporting both countries’ competing war efforts, the president who campaigned on ending the fighting in 24 hours is arguably helping prolong it. Talk about winning the battle but losing the war. If you’re like now-former Trump press secretary Katie Zacharia and suddenly have some free time on your hands, consider my colleagues’ suggestions about how to spend your weekend: Understand the world with the help of the Muppets: In 2012, Dahlia Lithwick published a piece of cultural analysis that permanently altered how readers viewed their marriages, their workplaces, and the U.S. Supreme Court. At its core, her theory reveals that everyone is either a freewheeling Chaos Muppet (think Gonzo) or a steady, conscientious Order Muppet (think Kermit), and the world runs on the balance between the two. To celebrate Slate’s 30th anniversary, Dahlia revisited the theory with longtime Slate favorite June Thomas for a special Plus-only episode of Amicus. Take a trip to Blorple Falls: Back in Slate’s earliest years, the magazine took a detour from covering the real world and decamped to a quirky town in “West Carolina” known as Blorple Falls, populated by fictitious personae like conservative congressman Blanton Foghorn and his pop-star niece Mindy D’Stasio. Why would Slate go to the trouble of creating this alternate reality? Katy Waldman went down the rabbit hole to excavate this weird chapter of internet history. Rethink your birthday plans: As a kid, you get a birthday party. But when you grow up, you’re stuck with a birthday dinner. In 2008, John Swansburg argued that we should ditch this particular festivity: “Seems like a nice idea, the birthday dinner. It is not. It is a tedious, wretched affair.” Get sucked into this quicksand investigation: Quicksand used to dominate pop culture, but its heyday is firmly behind us. How and why did the tide turn against this sticky substance? In 2010, Daniel Engber took a deep dive into quicksand, approaching the topic from such diverse angles as its depictions in film and TV, the fetish scene devoted to it, and its shifting role in political rhetoric. It’s been another busy week of news, and I, for one, am ready to take a breather. But rest assured, we’ll be back in your inbox with more wild happenings on Monday. Enjoy the weekend! Texas Just Accidentally Set Up a Great Experiment About Whether Facts Matter at All in 2026
2026-10-16

Texas Just Accidentally Set Up a Great Experiment About Whether Facts Matter at All in 2026

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. It seems, finally, that the strange episode involving James Talarico and a nonsense story about a mental health facility has concluded. The story’s greatest promoter, the man who claimed to hold the smoking-gun evidence showing the Senate candidate had been institutionalized, declared on Thursday that the matter was over. “Frankly, this entire situation has been blown completely out of proportion,” wrote the social X user Rhein Amacher, somewhat bafflingly, given his own singular efforts to blow the situation up. “I’m not going to speculate beyond the information available or continue fueling a controversy based on an unverified claim. As far as I’m concerned, this matter is closed. It’s time to move on to more substantive issues facing voters.” It was an odd statement, as it didn’t quite recant the story, which has been thoroughly debunked. (“Everyone can listen and draw their own conclusions,” he said.) But other anti-Talarico accounts went quiet on the matter after that, and the social media campaign to portray Talarico as a man in a state of emergency, too mentally ill to represent Texas in the Senate, fizzled out. Afterward, we were left to grapple with what this bizarre saga had meant. This conspiracy theory had blown up fast, taken over the discussion of the Texas Senate race for a week at a crucially important time, and died abruptly. In the world of political conspiracy theories, it can often be mysterious why some take off and others flounder. This one did something else, reaching extreme heights but never achieving the sustained success of more entrenched conspiracy theories. It may have been the blatant partisan hunger of this one that took it on this trajectory, or some other dynamic in the community that sought to prop it up. But whatever the reason, the outcome of this strange episode may tell us something important about the limits of political tactics involving big and reckless lies. By virtue of being everywhere and then falling apart so completely, the hoax has become a natural experiment in whether a ridiculous conspiracy theory can move the needle in a close election—even after it’s thoroughly debunked. If you missed this whole saga, it began with the flu. On Sept. 30, after days of in-person campaign events, Talarico made a virtual appearance at an event, saying he was sick. He then apparently came down with a pretty bad case of the flu and made no public appearances until Oct. 9, when he appeared at the Austin City Limits music festival. In that time, a story took off on the right. It began with small accounts on social media speculating or claiming that Talarico was out not because he was physically ill but because he was mentally ill. On Oct. 7, his opponent Ken Paxton released an ad saying Talarico was “missing” and appeared at a rally with Donald Trump in San Antonio. At that rally, he started a chant of “Where is James Talarico?” Over the next three days, the story grew, amplified by a number of conservative media outlets; Republican figures talking of “sources” in the know, and a guest on Steve Bannon’s War Room podcast. On Saturday, the first piece of “evidence” emerged to feed the narrative: Caroline Wren, a GOP fundraiser, posted flight records showing that on Oct. 9, the day of Talarico’s reappearance in Austin, a private plane had flown from from Ohio to Austin—suspicious, apparently, because the Ohio airport wasn’t far from a mental health facility where a Houston judge had sought treatment in 2023. This plane theory had first come from an X account called “TickleTexts,” who did not seem to consider that the Austin City Limits festival draws visitors from all over the country. But the story really shot off the next day when the MAGA influencer Nick Sortor, seizing on Wren’s starting point, posted an audio clip that seemed to be of a somewhat confusing phone call with the Ohio mental health facility, the Lindner Center for Hope. The phone call had come from Amacher—the man now complaining the situation has been blown out of proportion—who said he had called the center, pretending to be a member of Talarico’s family. In the recording, Amacher pressed an employee after she said there was no record of Talarico; she eventually said “it look[ed] like” Talarico had been discharged that same day. But that made no sense: Talarico had been seen at Austin City Limits the day before that. Still, there was a brief high, before people had worked out the impossibility of this claim, in which conservative Texas accounts celebrated their triumph, with some calling on Talarico to exit the race. Elon Musk promoted the story to his 241 million followers. But that evening, the Lindner Center of Hope posted on X to correct the record: Talarico had never been a patient. Talarico later provided home security footage to the New York Times that showed he had been home the entire time. Most players involved were forced to acknowledge the whole story had been nonsense. It was strikingly bad luck for Talarico to fall ill for so long so close to the campaign’s conclusion. (According to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, Talarico’s diabetes can make the flu worse.) But once it was revealed that the whole narrative was built on lies and fantasies from minor X accounts, puffed out with baseless conjecture and easily disprovable claims, the glee seemed to turn to a rare incidence of embarrassment among right-wing media. Writing on Tuesday, Fox News, evidently smarting from the incident, tried to deflect blame back onto Talarico. His “viral hospital rumor has been debunked,” the account wrote in a post accompanying an article about the debunking. “But the bigger story may be why his campaign sat on the evidence while the conspiracy theory exploded across social media for days.” We are at an age of remarkable ubiquity, speed, and traction of campaign conspiracy theories, made all the worse by the tech companies’ retreat from content moderation. Republican leaders and conservative media and influencers constantly encounter rumors like this; those who amplified the Talarico-hospital story chose to allow the loudness of its believers to determine its legitimacy, and not the facts. The easiest read on this whole episode is that Paxton’s supporters are anxious and desperate to hold onto a previously safely red and symbolically important state. Talarico has been polling slightly above Paxton recently, and Republicans have poured enormous amounts of money into the campaign. It certainly looks desperate, going big on a conspiracy theory that anyone clear-eyed about the situation must have known would inevitably be debunked. Paxton has a whole host of scandals; Talarico only has J.D. Vance implying Talarico is gay and the time Talarico called God nonbinary. It makes sense that Paxton’s side would be antsy. For now, it’s difficult to determine how much the conspiracy theory will matter in November’s election. It’s possible that it was almost entirely self-contained in conservative conversations on X—people who were never going to vote for Talarico anyway. It’s also possible that it went beyond that, and that voters who lacked either the time or the inclination to follow the story through to its debunking will go to the polls next month believing they are choosing between a candidate who spent a week in a mental institution and one who didn’t. In what’s forecast to be a close race, even a marginal number of such voters could matter. (There’s a third possibility, perhaps one too outlandish to take seriously. And that’s that Paxton and his team may pay some sort of penalty for pushing a story that fell apart instantly upon making contact with a shred of reason. The lesson of Trump’s decade atop American politics is that the penalty for lying is generally nonexistent, but this may just be the exception.) Which of those three possibilities—the story flopping, working, or backfiring—emerges is an important barometer of where we stand in 2026, and whether the truth still has any purchase in our modern era. If a couple of weeks from now we’re hearing that voters were turned off by their fear of Talarico’s fragility or instability, we’ll know things are worse than ever. But if this whole episode is dismissed and laughed off as the misadventure of a few desperate attention-seekers, then it’ll be a sign: Even in one of the hottest battlegrounds in the country, reality still matters. Cybercrims go beyond stealing data to attack physical building systems
2026-10-17

Cybercrims go beyond stealing data to attack physical building systems

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
'Hold my beer, have fun getting into your offices' Late Sunday night, a still-unknown cybercriminal or group hacked thousands of remote-controlled refrigeration systems across hundreds of French grocery stores, spiking temperatures, spoiling food, and sparking fires. This is part of an ongoing trend of attacking physical systems that can do damage in the real world. In August, a ransomware attack disrupted building-management systems at a Canadian hospital, affecting door access controls, central HVAC monitoring, and the issuing of staff access cards. And three years ago, cyberattacks hit Las Vegas casinos, causing slot machines to go dark, preventing payment kiosks and ATMs from spitting out cash, and locking guests out of their rooms when the electronic keys stopped working. Not all digital intrusions remain in cyberspace, and attackers don’t need to encrypt files and steal data to cause chaos. Hitting physical systems often produces more severe consequences - and turns up the heat on executives to pay an extortion demand. “There was one gig where the customer was refusing to pay, and so the threat actor was like, ‘Hold my beer, have fun getting into your offices,’ and locked everybody out,” Tim Beasley, a senior incident response consultant at Semperis, told The Register. Beasley won’t name the customer, or provide any incriminating information, but said the incident response involved police smashing glass in addition to his team recovering compromised data. “The cops had to break in, and literally get into the building, breaking some windows, so the customer was able to finally get back in,” he said. We asked if the customer paid the ransom demand, and Beasley yelled: “Oh, hell, no!” A report published earlier this month by ServiceNow’s Armis Labs found increasing evidence of ransomware attackers using physical consequences as leverage in their intrusions. “Today’s BAS operate at the intersection of IT and OT worlds, creating unprecedented exposure,” the report said. Building automation systems - BAS - are the critical systems that control and monitor facilities’ HVAC, lighting, energy usage, and water consumption. They also oversee badge readers, door locks, surveillance cameras, fire alarm control panels, and other physical safety equipment. As such, BAS make very attractive targets for ransomware operators looking for high-pressure ways to extort organizations into paying multi-million ransom demands. These systems also appeal to government-backed goons and hacktivists looking to ensure maximum psychological and physical impact during destructive cyberattacks. Armis Labs found 73 percent of organizations own BAS that have been used in ransomware attacks, and this same percentage have known exploited vulnerabilities in at least one of their building automation systems. Additionally, 91 percent of BAS communicate over insecure protocols - opening up an even bigger attack surface for cyber-miscreants wanting to cause physical harm. “The primary thing that we see with any attack on building management systems or physical systems: It’s a great place for the threat actor to hide,” Michael Freeman, ServiceNow’s senior director of threat research, told The Register. “It's easier for them to compromise, and it's easier for them to hide in.” BAS and operational technology (OT) in general have fewer security controls than IT systems. Most require continual uptime, which makes them harder to patch or even scan for vulnerabilities and misconfigurations as this may lead to outages. “One case in particular, against a very large financial institution, the threat actors were staying low and quiet by attacking their BMS systems, and then pivoting into the IT environment,” Freeman said. The intruders were routinely caught - and booted from - the financial institution’s IT systems, so they built backdoors into the BMS to ensure persistent access to both OT and IT networks. “We're also seeing threat actors using AI to understand more building automation systems, especially ones that are using obscure OSs like VxWorks, older Unix-type systems,” Freeman said. As multiple security experts have told us in previous interviews, security through obscurity is dead. Not only does AI make it easier for attackers to find bugs – some very obscure and decades old - but it also means criminals no longer need to be OT experts to carry out destructive cyberattacks on critical networks and facilities. Instead, they can use AI to learn all about the obscure protocols and proprietary hardware and software that historically made industrial control systems black boxes. “It’s easier to understand what the controls are,” Freeman said, using the example of an attacker trying to take down a plane, or some similar catastrophic cyberattack. Historically, even advanced, government-backed hacking squads would “be quiet, because you don't know what changing certain settings is going to do,” Freeman explained. “Now you can use AI to say, ‘If I compromise a weight and balance system for airlines, all I need to do now is move this number here to this number, and I just told the system to load heavy stuff on the right side of the plane instead of equal distribution.'” Freeman told us he has seen attackers break into airlines’ OT gear in the past. “But they didn’t know what they were getting into, and so they left them alone,” he said. “Those do cause me concern because in the past I've helped to kick people out of those types of systems.” OT security provider Dragos tracks ransomware groups’ claims against industrial organizations on the criminals’ leak sites, along with publicly disclosed intrusions affecting manufacturing and other critical sectors. To be clear: claims on leak sites aren’t always accurate or even true, and the claim itself doesn’t mean operations or control systems were hit. Since 2022, however, “the numbers have been consistently increasing,” Lexie Mooney, senior threat analyst at Dragos, told The Register. The cybersecurity firm tracked 995 ransomware attacks against industrial orgs in 2023, a 49.5 percent increase from 2022. That number jumped more than 87 percent in 2024. Active ransomware groups targeting industrial orgs also grew in 2024 to 80 groups, up from 50 the year before. Dragos tracked 119 ransomware groups impacting more than 3,300 industrial organizations in 2025, and the incidents have steadily increased every quarter this year: 1,020 incidents in the first quarter of 2026 and 1,140 incidents in Q2. “The vast majority of cases are opportunistic,” Abdul Alamri, a senior threat analyst at Dragos, told us. “In some of the cases that we have seen an operational impact, either physical systems or an OT operation, it’s just the nature of the opportunism on the part of those ransomware groups.” This is partially due to the complexity of OT systems, he added. “Ransomware groups want to disrupt operations, and put pressure on the victims to pay the ransom,” Alamri said. “They don’t want to invest that time and effort to target complex systems." It’s usually quicker and easier for financially motivated crims to affect operations via IT systems - as the extortionists did in the August 2025 Jaguar Land Rover (JLR) breach, which halted manufacturing and crippled business operations for months. The other side of this complexity, however, is that many organizations themselves still lack visibility into their OT environments. Dragos estimates that fewer than 10 percent of OT networks worldwide currently have sufficient network monitoring in place. Plus, in 30 percent of incidents the security firm responded to in 2025, the investigations did not begin with a detection alert, but with someone noticing that “something seemed wrong” in operations. This lack of visibility, combined with threats from AI, worries Alamri. “If you compare the risk that we have seen from humans, to what we are about to face in the near future with AI agents who might continuously try to break into those systems - it’s a pretty dark future if we don't take the right measures to protect ourselves against it.” As organizations get better at patching and securing IT systems, attackers will likely look to internet-connected OT systems where digital compromises have real-world consequences, according to Matt Hayden, VP of cyber and emerging threats at General Dynamics Information Technology. “We all anticipate that as we get better at reducing the IT low-hanging fruit, OT will become a vector of much more scrutiny and much more attention,” Hayden told The Register. Before joining GDIT, Hayden served as the assistant secretary for cyber, infrastructure, risk and resilience policy at the US Department of Homeland Security. “There is a big focus on the hardware being connected for the purpose of compliance - and to make sure that, in the name of compliance, we aren't opening back doors, to devices that traditionally wouldn't have to be connected to facilitate their service,” he said, using refrigeration units as an example of one such system that is now internet-connected for compliance purposes. This was prior to the French supermarket hacks. Refrigeration units’ monitoring systems connect to the internet so their owners and operators can prove to regulators that the units maintain their uptime and temperature in accordance with regulations. “So if the health department comes by, they say, ‘Look, my refrigerator was at 27 degrees for the last 72 days,' and they have a check mark, and they get their A plus. But that simple connectivity is also a vector in which that particular refrigerant unit could be targeted for disruption,” Hayden said. About a week after our interview, criminals did exactly that. “The recently reported exploitations of commercial refrigeration units represent a significant example of devices without digital value, no sensitive data or extended enterprise access, being exploited by being connected to the internet with unpatched firmware,” Hayden told us in a subsequent email. He said he expects the attribution to be “less nation-state, and more likely those looking for targets of opportunity to identify what could be done with the unpatched systems.” “The consequences are food supply degradation at a small scale, but it is an important example of what could happen at scale very quickly with more consequential operating equipment,” Hayden said.® Would you like personalized shopping recommendations based on that conversation you just had?
