Amazon wants you to Bee-lieve wearable AI can recall it all for you
2026-10-17

Amazon wants you to Bee-lieve wearable AI can recall it all for you

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Would you like personalized shopping recommendations based on that conversation you just had? Amazon wants to ensure you have a personal AI that you can carry with you anywhere, listening in on your conversations so it can remind you of key details – and give you personalized shopping recommendations, of course. The global online bazaar has unveiled two new AI-based products from Bee, a startup in wearable devices that it acquired last year in a deal said to be worth $300 million. Bee’s original product for Amazon was tried out and sort of given approval by friend of The Register Corey Quinn, Chief Cloud Economist at Duckbill, earlier this year. One new gizmo, Bee Frontier, is designed to be worn like a watch or one of those fitness trackers, although to our mind it looks more like you are wearing a doorbell. The other, Bee Eclipse, is a slimline and lightweight device about the size of a credit card that attaches to the back of your phone magnetically, like a limpet. Both are intended to listen in to your conversations and help you remember important details, like when you are meeting your friend for a pint later and at which hospitality outlet. The devices take note of the details you might otherwise forget, and then turn them into useful conversation summaries, to-dos and reminders, Amazon says. The pair of devices feature a single button to turn on or off, and a visible indicator that lights up when one is listening in and its AI is summarizing the conversation. When enabled, the eavesdropper gathers context from your conversations to provide insights and recommendations. Summaries of your conversations can be accessed via a Bee mobile app on iPhone or Android (linked via Bluetooth), or an all-new desktop app. These can be organized using templates for things such as lectures, or users can just review memories from the past few days to see what you discussed. The Bee devices pick up on trends or themes that recur, and recommend personalized goals and actions. That sounds alarm bells for us, as it suggests the AI is designed to get to know you, and what you like. Along with the new hardware, Bee has now gained the ability to connect to Amazon’s Alexa+ AI assistant and Amazon.com, which means that you can ask Alexa for a meeting summary - or get personalized shopping recommendations from Amazon based on what your personal earwig has gleaned. However, Amazon says that the latest Bee products respect user privacy, as audio is processed into insights in real time and not stored, while transcripts are encrypted and only the user can access them. Bee Frontier is claimed to have IP67 water resistance, up to seven days of battery life, and has a USB-C charging port. Bee Eclipse also has a USB-C port but is said to last for more than 21 days of use from a single battery charge. Both devices come in a choice of colors, with Bee Frontier available now for $74.99 and Bee Eclipse due to be available shortly for $99.99. ® Would you like personalized shopping recommendations based on that conversation you just had? Get in the bin! UK Space Agency wants public to name its debris removal mission
2026-10-17

Get in the bin! UK Space Agency wants public to name its debris removal mission

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
ClearSpace UK aims to be operational in 2029 with public's suggestion etched on the side of the spacecraft The UK Space Agency needs a name for its Active Debris Removal Mission and has decided that the great British public are the people for the job. The agency confirmed the operation earlier this week and selected ClearSpace UK to lead it. The plan is to remove two UK-registered satellites from Low Earth Orbit, with the clean-up due to begin in 2029. The UK government is backing the effort with more than £60 million, and it expects the disposal to be completed by 2032. The spacecraft will grab each satellite, drop it into a lower orbit for disposal by burning up in the Earth's atmosphe, then climb to a higher one to await its next job. Less suicide dive, more cosmic bin round. And because it’s refuellable, the UK government says it "can help make a difference and sweep space for years to come." Launch is planned from SaxaVord Spaceport in Scotland. But first, the quest needs a name. The Active Debris Removal Mission hardly trips off the tongue, and the UK Space Agency gave inspirational examples of other project names, such as NASA's Mercury program (named after the Roman god) or Tim Peake's Principia mission (inspired by Sir Isaac Newton's Philosophiæ Naturalis Principia Mathematica). Oddly, the agency didn't mention the poll to brand a scientific research ship, which was eventually named Sir David Attenborough rather than Boaty McBoatface, the most popular suggestion. The tag must consist of two words – so no "Itsy bitsy teeny weeny orbit debris clear machiney" – and 150 words to explain the name and how it reflects the theme. So no backronyms, for example Broom (British Robotic Orbital Object Manipulator). Starry McBinface? Obviously, swear words are not allowed, nor is anything deemed "potentially offensive" or political. Using anything that might attract copyright or intellectual property lawyers is also out. Letting the public suggest a name feels potentially riskier than the mission itself, though it should generate some engagement. It's a high-risk, high-reward campaign; there are plenty of dead satellites cluttering orbit, and the ability to remove them is becoming increasingly important. Rob Atchison, Managing Director of ClearSpace UK, said, "Our next step is to design, build and test a spacecraft that can get close, safely, to an object never designed to be caught, and take control of it." If you're getting a sense of déjà vu about this expedition, you're probably right. The European Space Agency (ESA) announced ClearSpace-1 in 2019, which the agency called "the first space mission to remove an item of debris from orbit" with a launch set for 2025. At the time, ClearSpace founder and CEO Luc Piguet said: "This is the right time for such a mission." In latest announcement, Piguet called the UKSA's selection "an important milestone." This is far from the first mission to grapple an object in orbit. Most recently, Katalyst Space sent its LINK spacecraft to rescue NASA's Swift observatory. That was intended to capture the Swift spacecraft and raise its altitude, but failures with LINK doomed it. Perhaps things might have gone better if the public had been given an opportunity to name the spacecraft? ® Would you like personalized shopping recommendations based on that conversation you just had? Open-weight models win hearts, minds, and wallets
2026-10-16

Open-weight models win hearts, minds, and wallets

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
They handle 71.5% of Vercel gateway tokens and nearly a quarter of estimated spending Open-weight models more than quadrupled their share of spending through Vercel's AI Gateway between June and September, when they processed more than seven in 10 tokens. "In August, open-weight models processed the majority of AI Gateway token volume for the first time," the biz said. "The money is now following. Open weight's share of spend has more than quadrupled over the last three months, from one in twenty gateway dollars in June to nearly one in four in September." AI Gateway, like OpenRouter, acts as an intermediary that allows customers to purchase tokens from different AI providers through a common interface. While it works with popular harnesses like Claude Code and Codex and provides access to proprietary and open-weight models, it doesn't see usage through other gateways or from customers who access AI models directly from vendors or through on-premises hosting. While gateway spending in September was about 13x higher than recorded in December 2025, the average price per token was about 47 percent lower, according to Vercel. At the same time, almost one in three teams changed their most-used AI provider in September, suggesting that market competition may be limiting vendor lock-in. The most popular model in September was TypeSafe AI's Jev. In just 14 days, it reached 35 percent of paid teams using Vercel's AI Gateway, the company said. Its popularity didn't generate much spending due to the model's architecture – usage accounted for only 1 percent of the month’s tokens and 0.15 percent of spending. US-based models from Anthropic and OpenAI still attract the largest portion of spending in September, with 11.03 percent going to Claude Opus 5.5, followed by GPT-6 Astra at 4.86 percent. But models from Chinese AI providers have become more popular. DeepSeek V4.1 Flash ranked third in spending, at 4.19 percent. More than two-thirds of the September spending on Chinese models went to US providers serving those models, up from less than a tenth in December 2025, Vercel said. Moonshot AI, maker of Kimi, captured 3.6 percent of gateway tokens and 13.2 percent of spend in September; DeepSeek took 58 percent of tokens for just 6.6 percent of spend; and Z.ai, maker of GLM-5.3, took 7.2 percent of tokens and 4.5 percent of spend. So efforts to ban or limit open-weight models, something considered by the current US administration, would affect American businesses serving those models and their customers. Even if the US continues to dither on AI policy, trade barriers affecting AI models may alter market dynamics. China on Friday proposed adding 40 technology items to its export control list, 23 of them related to AI. The Associated Press notes that Moonshot’s Kimi K3 and Alibaba’s Qwen 3.8-Max are among the models that meet the parameter threshold set by Chinese authorities, though the official message did not identify affected models. "This introduces significant uncertainty for US businesses that host or rely on Chinese models," Chris McGuire, a senior fellow for China and emerging technologies at the Council on Foreign Relations, said in a social media post. "Particularly given that Chinese AI labs are moving toward licensing the use of open-source models to generate revenue, the Chinese government stating that it has the ability to curtail those licensing agreements at any time should make US businesses more hesitant to rely on those models going forward." McGuire said the US needs to adopt a comprehensive regulatory framework governing the use of US AI technology overseas and include Chinese AI models used or hosted in the United States in its domestic AI regulations.® Would you like personalized shopping recommendations based on that conversation you just had? Benchmarked: Nvidia's RTX Spark notebook delivers serious local AI compute, at a price
2026-10-16

Benchmarked: Nvidia's RTX Spark notebook delivers serious local AI compute, at a price

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
El Reg digs its claws into HP's N1X-powered OmniBook Ultra 16 Nvidia's RTX Spark partner systems are here, and spec-for-spec they've got all the ingredients for a hit: a potent CPU, blisteringly fast integrated graphics, and up to 128 GB of unified memory in a lightweight, ultra-premium package that's ready to run demanding models locally. But with prices for these systems starting around $2,600 and going well north of $6,000 for the top spec trims, the one thing RTX Spark isn't is a good deal. However, for the right person, by which we mostly mean AI enthusiasts, researchers, and/or creative professionals who need tons of video memory and don't want to get lost in Apple's walled garden or would prefer Nvidia's CUDA ecosystem over AMD's ROCm, the RTX Spark still has a lot to offer. As such, we'll be largely focusing on the local agentic AI use cases Nvidia and Microsoft are pushing for these "builder-class" systems. The Register had the opportunity this week to put the OmniBook Ultra, HP's unique spin on the RTX Spark platform, through a series of AI benchmarks to see just how well it performs. And spoiler alert: you'll be hard pressed to find a faster Windows notebook for local AI. But before diving into performance, let's take a closer look at the hardware itself. Lifting the lid on the OmniBook Ultra 16, the first thing you'll notice is the gorgeous 2880x1800 pixel OLED touchscreen with a 16:10 aspect ratio. Touch screens on professional notebooks can be a contentious topic. You either love them or hate them, but at the end of the day, if the idea of fingerprints obscuring your work gives you pause, no one is forcing you to use it, and we're happy to see it here. The OmniBook Ultra's display is mounted to a surprisingly stiff hinge mechanism mounted to the top of the chassis. This has the benefit of keeping the hinge out of the way of the notebook's ample exhaust, which is kind of important because the 20-core N1X system-on-chip powering our review unit runs hot. The system's ample venting keeps most of that heat concentrated around the base of the display and for the most part, away from the keyboard. Speaking of keyboards, the OmniBook Ultra's digit fidgets are a bit of a throwback. Instead of the now-ubiquitous chiclet keys popularized by Apple in the early '10s, HP has gone for a more traditional notebook keyboard, which we found refreshingly easy to type on. Keyboards of course are subjective so if there's any question on whether this one is right for you, it might be worth a trip to your local big box store to give one a good pecking before you commit. Framing the keyboard are a pair of speaker grilles backed by four Cirrus Logic amplifiers which sound excellent for a Windows notebook, but can't quite match the clarity and bass of Apple's MacBook Pros. With that said, we don't expect anyone will be disappointed by the OmniBook Ultra's speakers. Directly below the keyboard is a haptic trackpad that is as precise as it is enormous. No product in this price class should be shipping with a diving board mechanism in 2026, so it's nice to see HP didn't cheap out on us. Moving to the sides of the notebook, you'll find a refreshingly comprehensive set of ports including: Our only real critique here is that the OmniBook Ultra, like other laptops, lacks a full size SD card slot. MicroSD cards are convenient, but they're not widely used in professional environments and we'd have preferred to see a full size SD card reader or better yet a CFexpress card reader. However, it's rare if not impossible to see these on notebooks. The chassis itself weighs in at 4.89 pounds and is milled from aluminum with a remarkably premium-feeling finish on the palm rest, lid, and bottom panels. HP has done a nice job building a notebook that feels as premium as it is powerful. While the OmniBook Ultra is an objectively nice machine, the main attraction of any of the RTX Spark systems is going to be the N1X chip powering them. The SoC features a MediaTek-derived CPU chiplet that's melded using advanced packaging and Nvidia's NVLink Fusion chip-to-chip interconnect to a Blackwell GPU. The N1X comes in two flavors: An entry level version with 18 CPU cores and a GPU with 5,120 CUDAs. Meanwhile, the highest-end SKU — the one found in our review unit — features up to 20 CPU cores and a GPU with 6,144 CUDA cores. If you're wondering, that's exactly the same number as found in Nvidia's RTX 5070 just with access to a whole lot more memory. We'll note that not all of those CPU cores are created equal. Like most Arm-based SoCs, the N1X contains a mix of big and little cores. Specifically, it has an equal complement of Arm X925 and Cortex A725 cores. While all of this sounds impressive, it's worth noting that the chip really isn't all that new. The N1X is based heavily on the GB10, which launched in the Linux-based DGX Spark over a year ago. With that said, there have been some improvements. At Hot Chips 2025, we learned the GB10 would support memory up to 9,400 MT/s. But, when the DGX Spark finally arrived that fall, it was only sporting 8,533 MT/s memory. The N1X rectifies this by boosting memory bandwidth to around 300 GB/s. Higher memory bandwidth usually translates to faster token generation in AI inference and agentic workloads. So, while a fairly modest boost in bandwidth over the DGX Spark's 273 GB/s, it's still a meaningful one. There aren't many modern workloads that really need 128 GB of unified memory, but local AI certainly is one of them. While you can certainly get away with less, it opens the door to models with a decent shot at replacing something like Claude Code or Codex. In fact, Microsoft believes that the future of Windows will be that of Copilot+ PCs that can run a mix of local and remote models with prompts routed between them based on things like complexity or privacy. Redmond is calling this technique hybrid intelligence. But even with 128 GB of shared memory on board, you're still somewhat limited on what models can actually fit. That's because, unlike on Linux, which allocates memory dynamically to the DGX Spark's GPU as needed, Windows isn't quite asur testi smart. The HP OmniBook Ultra arrived with a 50:50 split with 64 GB dedicated to the CPU and GPU respectively. This can be reconfigured as needed, but it's not exactly ideal. The good news is that, with the right software, you can get around this. Llama.cpp, one of the most popular local LLM inference frameworks, allows for overflowing into CPU memory as needed and, since the CPU and GPU share the same memory, the performance penalty isn't nearly as stark as it would when using a dedicated GPU. El Reg is excited to bring you more hardware coverage like this, but we're only just getting started. Ideally we'd pit the RTX Spark-based OmniBook Ultra against a similarly specced AMD Strix Halo notebook or Apple M5 Max MacBook Pro. Unfortunately we don't have those systems in our database just yet. In their place we're including performance figures for the DGX Spark and HP's Strix Halo-based Z2-Mini G1A. But because those aren't notebooks, we recognize it's not an ideal comparison. Having said that, you have to fit a lot more than just the model's weights into memory. For agentic AI harnesses like Claude Code, models need to support large context windows with 128,000 tokens being a sweet spot these days. Depending on the model, that can chew up an awful lot of memory. There is also the issue of the available models. While you could theoretically cram a 200 billion parameter model onto an RTX Spark using Llama.cpp, there aren't really many models that size. Since Nvidia's RTX Spark platform ships with Windows, we conducted all of our tests using the v0.6.0 build of Llama.cpp across five models developed by Meta, Google, and Alibaba in sizes ranging from around 26 to 122 billion parameters. Our first test simulates a chatbot-like environment tasking each system and model to process and generate a 256 token input and output. There are two metrics worth paying attention to here: Time to first token (TTFT) measures the time in milliseconds required for the GPU to process the prompt and generally reflects the GPU's compute performance. Tokens per second (Tok/s) is a little more obvious and reflects how quickly the system can churn out tokens once the prompt has been processed. Also called decode, token generation is usually dependent on memory bandwidth, so faster memory tends to perform better here. Across our test suite, we see performance scales neatly with model size and active parameter count. Pitted against AMD's Strix Halo and Nvidia's earlier DGX Spark systems, which, again, aren't ideal comparison points since they're mini PCs with more robust cooling systems, the HP OmniBook Ultra performs quite well. In Qwen3.6-35B-A3B — the fastest model we tested on any of the systems — the N1X's faster memory did give it an edge over the DGX Spark in token generation. The GB10 does appear to offer slightly higher compute performance, perhaps due to better thermals. Both Nvidia-based systems offer better performance than AMD's Strix Halo platform in our tests, though this shouldn't come as a surprise. On paper, Strix Halo offers less performance and memory bandwidth than either Nvidia chip and we've repeatedly shown this in testing over the past year. AMD's saving grace is that it has consistently offered better value. While our single batch and context scaling benchmarks are useful for quantifying system performance across models, they aren't necessarily representative of how you're likely to use local models today. If our numbers seem a little low compared to other benchmarks for the N1X and GB10, that's because our single batch results don't take advantage of prefill caching or speculative decoding. If you're not familiar, prefill caching helps to speed up prompt processing time for information the model has already seen before. Speculative decoding meanwhile involves using a small draft model to predict the outputs of a larger one. In highly repetitive workloads, like agentic coding, speculative decoding speeds up token generation by 2-3x without any loss in quality. AI agents, like code assistants, pass over the same prompts, code bases, and resources recursively, which makes them prime candidates for both techniques. In real world benchmarks, the overall line up remains unchanged to see high overall performance from these systems. This test measures system performance when running an agentic coding workflow using Alibaba's Qwen Code (not to be confused with the Qwen-Coder family of models) harness and measures how quickly each system and model can solve a bin packing problem. This addresses one of the more interesting phenomena that has cropped up around agentic AI. Faster token generation doesn't necessarily translate to a faster time to solve. Smarter but slower models can still be faster if they use fewer tokens to arrive at the correct answer. And we see that here. Large models like Qwen3.5-122B-A10B ran slower on our test systems, generating tokens at roughly half the rate of the smaller 35B parameter model. Yet, the median time to solve was roughly 40 seconds faster on the larger model because it needed only a fraction of the tokens. Having said that, given the extreme cost of memory right now, we believe the sweet spot for local AI code assistants is somewhere in the 20-60 billion parameter range, which is small enough to run on less expensive 64 GB versions of Nvidia's RTX Spark systems. As you might expect, any new platform is going to have a few growing pains at launch. It was certainly true when Intel introduced its performance and efficiency cores and when Qualcomm brought its Arm-based Snapdragon X Elite SoCs to the PC. Nvidia's RTX Spark platform is no different. During our testing, we observed issues with thermal throttling or rather an apparent lack thereof. Under a constant AI inference load, we experienced occasional lock ups and, on one occasion, graphical artifacts as the GPU exceeded 90 degrees Celsius. AI inference and training are some of the most resource-intensive workloads ever developed and to call them resource viruses wouldn't be inaccurate. So it's not surprising that a sustained inference load over the course of several hours might overwhelm the thin-and-light's thermal management system. Under real world conditions we don't expect this will be an issue. Even a few seconds between prompts was enough for the notebook to remain stable and artifact free. With that said, if you were hoping to fine tune LLMs, you might want to look at Nvidia's DGX Spark or any one of the other GB10-based AI workstations from HP, Dell, Lenovo, and others. Their larger chassis and thermal management systems make them far better candidates for the often hours-long process of fine tuning models. Software support for PyTorch also happens to be a bit better on Linux than on Windows. Beyond a cooling system that's perhaps a bit optimistic for what is supposed to be a 140 watt SoC, the only glitch we ran into was the Cirrus Logic audio outputs disabling themselves out of the blue. Thankfully all it took to get them running again was to uninstall them in device manager and reboot. The OmniBook Ultra 16 and the rest of the RTX Spark lineup occupy a rather niche segment in 2026. With memory prices at an all time high, the system spans the gamut from ultra-premium thin-and-light in its base config and mobile workstation at the upper end of the spectrum. The OmniBook Ultra 16 is an objectively strong performer and an extremely well built one at that. But at around $3,200 for the base model OmniBook Ultra 16 with the N1X and $6,229 as tested here, none of these systems are cheap. Had Nvidia and its partners launched these notebooks a year ago at between $2,000 and $4,000, the story might have been different. Unless you've drunk the AI Kool-Aid and are buying an RTX Spark system for local AI development or happen to have a particularly VRAM hungry workload that makes you money, these systems are going to be a tough sell for the same reason it's hard to recommend workstation components to the average consumer. Most folks can't take full advantage of them and their money is usually better spent elsewhere. And if you can and you're willing to give up a little performance, AMD's Strix and Gorgon Halo-based notebooks and mini PCs are definitely worth cross shopping. As memory prices normalize, we do expect Nvidia's next-generation of RTX Spark systems to be both faster and more attractively priced. Unfortunately, we will wait a while for that to happen. The memory fabs expect DRAM supplies to remain constrained through at least 2028. We've got a whole lot more testing planned for the RTX Spark and HP's OmniBook Ultra, including a comprehensive set of CPU and gaming benchmarks, along with a deeper dive into agentic AI performance as we look to answer the question whether these systems can actually save you money. So far that argument isn't looking good. ® 'Hold my beer, have fun getting into your offices' Would you like personalized shopping recommendations based on that conversation you just had? Node.js creator ditches Deno to work on open source Cloudflare Workers
2026-10-16

Node.js creator ditches Deno to work on open source Cloudflare Workers

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
You're building servers 'wrong,' says Ryan Dahl as Cloudflare gobbles his Deno startup Node.js creator Ryan Dahl is sacrificing a battle to win the war … for server-side computing and allowing Node-replacement Deno to fall by the wayside. Last week, Cloudflare announced that it had purchased Dahl’s company Deno, which was built around an open source server-side JavaScript runtime of the same name that he created, to compete with the Node.js runtime, which he also created. Nonetheless, Deno has no future at Cloudflare, which will maintain the runtime for another year before ending its development. "I am now of the opinion that if you're choosing Node.js for building servers, you’re doing it wrong," Dahl told The Register. For Cloudflare, the Deno buy is an acquihire for Dahl and 12 of his Deno colleagues, who will work to build out Cloudflare’s Workers platform, expanding it for potential open-source use. He feels this approach is the way to go. “Right now, every other server is really operating at the Linux VM layer. That is not what Cloudflare Workers are," he said. Dahl’s Node.js, which he presented to the world in 2009, sparked a revolution in Web application architecture. He was fed up with the Apache web server hogging all the memory.  Using the Google V8 JavaScript engine, Node not only could execute JavaScript code on the server, but it could concurrently serve thousands of requests per second with a fraction of the memory required by the traditional choice of the time, Apache Web server. Today, Node powers about 30 million websites worldwide. With 130 million downloads, it probably runs a lot more internal operations as well. The ever-fickle Dahl first debuted Deno in 2018 as a rewrite of Node. The name ‘Deno’ is an anagram of Node, and it was Dahl’s attempt to fix everything he felt he got wrong with Node – built-in fetch, a permission model, native TypeScript support et al. Sequoia backed Dahl’s company for $21 million in 2022, and as of August, Deno amassed 400,000 monthly users. Nonetheless, Node’s gravity proved too heavy for Deno to achieve escape velocity. “What we found over years of working on Deno is that you can't really deviate from Node.js very much," Dahl told The Register. "As we continued to try to get adoption for Deno, we found we had to essentially backpedal on some of the original promises and become more Node compatible over time, and that left us as a kind of an alternative Node.js implementation.” Going forward, Cloudflare will supply Deno with bug fixes and security patches for the next year, while Dahl looks for a new home for the MIT-licensed open source project (the OpenJS Foundation perhaps?). In August, Dahl released an entirely new project, called celld, which was an open source implementation of Cloudflare’s Durable Objects. He was fascinated by the technology, originally created by Kenton Varda. “A primitive this good deserves to run anywhere,” the celld page stated. Durable Objects allow developers to build stateful, real-time distributed applications. Unlike Node or Deno, the resulting application doesn’t require a dedicated runtime environment. It runs on Cloudflare’s serverless Workers runtime, which executes code inside Google JavaScript V8 isolates — a lightweight sandbox running within a single OS process. Individual instances of the application can be spun up at an edge location closest to the user. Each Durable Object instance acts as a single-threaded micro-server with a unique global ID and its own co-located storage, where user data for the session is stored in its own dedicated copy of SQLite. Durable Objects “is kind of the perfect distributed systems abstraction,” Dahl said. The drawback of Durable Objects for Dahl was that it was hard-wired into Cloudflare’s infrastructure. He created celld as an implementation that could be hosted anywhere, not just on Cloudflare. As it turns out, Cloudflare had similar aspirations. In 2022, the company had open-sourced a version of the Workers runtime, called workerd, but the release was more geared towards developer testing. The company never got around to making Workers fully self hosting, much less giving it the chops to maintain distributed state, a power Dahl gave to celld. So the mission for Dahl's team is to merge the work of celld with workerd for a stand-alone JavaScript/WebAssembly runtime, while integrating it with Workers and Durable Objects as much as possible. (celld is also going bye-bye.) Such a technology could be yet another revolutionary turn for server-side JavaScript, offering a runtime that would require only 300 KB of memory per instance, rather than the hundreds of megabytes now required for Node/Deno. Such savings could quickly add up for a cloud provider, especially one serving the voracious agentic market. But until we see the fruits of Dahl’s team, the market for independent runtimes will be squeezed, what with the absence of Deno and Anthropic’s recent acquisition (and rewriting) of Bun. Now, Dahl’s first creation, the mighty Node, will have to go it alone.® Would you like personalized shopping recommendations based on that conversation you just had? The Verge
Mercedes-AMG’s first ground-up SUV is a 1,150hp electric beast
2026-10-17

Mercedes-AMG’s first ground-up SUV is a 1,150hp electric beast

The Verge | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
It may even out-muscle the Porsche Cayenne Turbo Electric and Lucid Gravity. It may even out-muscle the Porsche Cayenne Turbo Electric and Lucid Gravity. Earlier this year, Mercedes’ AMG performance brand grabbed our attention with the GT four-door coupe, a souped-up sports sedan with three axial flux motors delivering over 1,150 horsepower. Well, now meet its beefier cousin, the 2028 Mercedes-AMG GT SUV, an equally powerful, ultra-fast-charging electric people-hauler. The AMG GT SUV (say that five times fast) marks the first time in the performance brand’s 59-year history that it has developed a full-size SUV entirely in-house from the ground up. Scheduled to hit the US next summer, the new EV is built on Merc’s high-performance AMG.EA architecture to combine sports-car handling with full-size SUV suburban practicality. It will be available in three trims — GT 53, GT 55, and the flagship GT 63 — with the top model delivering 1,153hp via the same three axial flux motors powering the four-door coupe. That beats the Porsche Cayenne Turbo Electric with Launch Control, which is sure to make for some awkward run-ins around Stuttgart. Thanks to an 800-volt architecture, the SUV supports blistering-fast charging speeds up to 710kW on the 128kWh battery (GT 63 / GT 55) and 600kW on the 106kWh battery (GT 53). Drivers can charge 10–80 percent in 12 minutes or gain up to 450km (around 280 miles) of WLTP range in just 10 minutes. The 128kWh battery trims will have an estimated max range of 435 miles. That battery, by the way? It utilizes F1-inspired technology with specialized NCMA cathodes, silicon-containing anodes, and cylindrical cells. An electrically nonconductive oil flows directly around each individual cell for uniform thermal management, enabling continuous high-power delivery and long battery life. Mercedes has quietly been on the vanguard of battery technology for a while now, with the AMG GT SUV just the latest example. The 710kW charging speed, in particular, could give Mercedes another edge over Porsche’s 320kW speeds. For a full-size SUV, the AMG GT sounds like it could handle like a vehicle half its size. Mercedes-AMG replaced the traditional mechanical roll bars with a newly developed suspension that uses active hydraulic elements to eliminate pitch and roll during acceleration, heavy braking, and cornering. Combined with three-stage air springs and standard six-degree active rear-axle steering, it purports to balance track precision with long-distance comfort. But just because it’s a beefy lad doesn’t mean it’ll tip the scales. The AMG GT 63 SUV weighs in at 5,941lbs, making it slightly lighter than the Lotus Eletre R (6,007lbs) and Lucid Gravity (6,100lbs), and nearly identical to the Porsche Cayenne Turbo Electric (5,960lbs). Silence is golden — except to a performance brand’s EV engineering team. That means piping in recorded sounds of real AMGs — in this case, the legendary AMG V8 and six-cylinder engines — to preserve the “emotional” AMG identity. Mercedes is proud that it’s using real engine sounds over synthetic EV tones, though some may disagree. The system even simulates mechanical gear shifts with paddle controls, offers adjustable sound profiles, and integrates physical seat vibrations — so you can still feel like there’s a real engine under that hood instead of a 78-liter frunk. The so-called AMGFORCE Sport+ drive program is offered as standard across all three trims of the Mercedes-AMG GT SUV. Inside, you’ll find the MB.OS platform on your 14-inch display, with an army of AI agents from ChatGPT, Microsoft, and Google at your disposal to boss around. Use them to set navigation, discover random locations, do web searches, tell bad jokes, or whatever else people use AI for in their cars. Merc’s own onboard AI system can handle the physical stuff, like adjusting HVAC, changing drive modes, or fiddling with the lighting — all through voice commands. Beyond voice and navigation, the AMG GT SUV applies AI to high-performance track driving through something called the Predictive Performance Manager. Mercedes says the PPM uses track-mapping data to dynamically throttle or boost electric motor output depending on topography, conserving battery energy on downhill or technical winding sections so full power is available on steep inclines and long straights. That could also mean faster, more consistent overall lap times by eliminating battery overheating and power throttling during track driving — something an AMG lover would likely appreciate. Mercedes didn’t release the official pricing yet, though we can expect something closer to its official launch. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Elon Musk settles lawsuits over $1 million voter giveaway

The Verge | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Plaintiffs claiming Musk’s $1 million voter lottery was a fraud are settling for an undisclosed sum. Plaintiffs claiming Musk’s $1 million voter lottery was a fraud are settling for an undisclosed sum. In 2024, Elon Musk promised to give away $1 million a day to a randomly selected registered voter in a swing state. That prompted legal challenges, raised questions about its legality, and drew lawsuits from two Arizona residents claiming the lottery was a fraud. Musk has now agreed to settle the two lawsuits filed in Austin, Texas, though the details of the settlement have not been made public. The plaintiffs claim that when Musk announced the sweepstakes, he falsely claimed the prizes would be awarded randomly. Philadelphia’s District Attorney sued to stop the giveaway, claiming that Musk was running an illegal lottery. During a hearing, Chris Gober, a representative for Musk’s America PAC, told the judge that “The $1 million recipients are not chosen by chance … We know exactly who will be announced as the $1 million recipient today and tomorrow.” That directly contradicted Musk’s claim that the drawings were random. The plaintiffs say they never would have signed the petition required to enter the giveaway if they had known it wasn’t random. They accused Musk and America PAC of using it as a ploy to obtain user data and said the petition “places no limitations on America PAC’s use of or sale of the personal data it collects, nor does it provide any additional information about the planned use of data.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Meta removes language that may have allowed it to scrape news sites
2026-10-17

Meta removes language that may have allowed it to scrape news sites

The Verge | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Language that experts say allowed the scraping of news sites to train its AI has been removed from Meta’s terms. Language that experts say allowed the scraping of news sites to train its AI has been removed from Meta’s terms. After backlash, Meta is updating its terms again to remove language that suggested it could scrape content from sites, including news sites, to train its AI. Specifically, the following language no longer appears on the commercial terms page: … Meta may use any content from such domains, web pages, or URLs to provide, develop, improve, research, train, and maintain the integrity of, the Meta Company Products, services, and features provided under these Commercial Terms and any other applicable supplemental terms. Many, including Neiman Lab and James Grimmelmann, a professor of digital and information law at Cornell, interpreted it as an attempt to give Meta the right to use content scraped from sites to train its AI products for free and supersede blocking instructions in a site’s robots.txt file. Andy Stone, Meta’s vice president of communications, and spokesperson Daniel Roberts pushed back, saying that “There is no change to how Meta uses web crawlers; any suggestion to the contrary is false.” Today’s update to its terms and a new statement go further, removing the controversial language entirely. Roberts said in a follow-up statement to The Verge this morning: “We’ve heard you and want to clarify: the crawling terms we consolidated only applied to our ads and business products, and we’ve clarified that in the terms themselves. It does not change how Meta uses web crawlers. As always, content owners can use robots.txt to signal their preferences. This is not about scraping news sites, and these updates don’t give us new rights that supersede those preferences.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Jad Abumrad loves a butterfly knife and a good springy pillow
2026-10-17

Jad Abumrad loves a butterfly knife and a good springy pillow

The Verge | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
The Radiolab creator and host of Dolly Parton’s America answers The Verge’s weekend questionnaire. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. The Radiolab creator and host of Dolly Parton’s America answers The Verge’s weekend questionnaire. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. It’s hard to overstate Jad Abumrad’s impact on the podcasting world. He created and hosted Dolly Parton’s America and Fela Kuti: Fear No Man, two Peabody Award-winning shows that offered unprecedented looks at the lives of two of the most important music icons of the last century. Before that, though, he created Radiolab, an experimental NPR show that leaned heavily on creative sound design and eventually helped redefine what a podcast could be. In the early days of podcasting, shows were often just a couple of people talking at each other at a table (some still are), or lazily repackaged radio broadcasts. Radiolab instead told deeply reported stories in themed episodes with original music and cinematic sound effects that helped enhance the storytelling. And often, the sounds and music were created by Abumrad himself. Abumrad retired from Radiolab in 2022, leaving it in the capable hands of Latif Nasser and Lulu Miller. But he’s been plenty busy since then, not only producing Fear No Man, but also composing for film, theater, and dance, and serving as a distinguished research professor at Vanderbilt University. Despite his intimidating resume, however, he’s a man of simple pleasures who collects an ungodly amount of audio plug-ins and loves the satisfying clack of a butterfly knife. My pillow. I need a flat, springy pillow, and I don’t understand this new hotel / airbnb trend towards giant fluffy pillows that wreck your neck for a week (I’m that age). So, a good travel pillow. In terms of digital tools: it’s rare to say you love a calendar app, but I truly love Fantastical. It takes the most annoying task in the world — scheduling a meeting with many busy people — and makes it actually fun. In terms of digital audio tools: I am addicted to audio plugins and have a thousand too many, but the one I come back to over and over is a little granular synthesis app called Granite. It’s 15 years old at this point, but it’s just so musical. I’ve used it to score dozens of OG Radiolab pieces. Which is the most underappreciated?I think indoor plumbing is wildly underappreciated. I have a whole talk I give about the miracle of indoor plumbing. What is the first app you install on a new phone or computer?Alfred. It’s a super lightweight do-everything replacement for Apple’s built-in Spotlight. Like Raycast but without the annoying AI features. What is one thing you wish you could change about your phone?I wish I could eliminate all the buzzes and dings. In fact, I wish there were a button that could turn my smartphone into a very dumb phone, because I only need it to be smart a few minutes a day. What sites do you have pinned to your tab bar?My tab bar is boring and functional: NYT, Ground.News, the Vanderbilt Library resource page (which links to Nexis and all the journals), ESPN. How many tabs do you have open right now?14, but today has been a big research day. Which social media platform do you use the most (if any)?I use Instagram sometimes. I used to be active on Twitter and had a decent following, pre-Elon, but then they changed the login process, and I got locked out. I never could get back in and stopped wanting to. What is your happy place online?Bandcamp, Andrew Huang’s YouTube channel. What is your favorite gadget you’ve ever owned?A tie between my Motorola flip phone from 1997 and a butterfly knife I saved up for a decade earlier. Snapping open and shut the phone satisfied some deep, restless urge, and the same was true for the rotational flourish of the wrist involved in opening and closing the knife. Which was the most disappointing?Muse brainwave headset. What game do you have the fondest memories of?Around the same time as the butterfly knife, I used to save up quarters all week and then run to the local Kroger and play Donkey Kong. I was never good. But I do have a distinct memory of one time getting to a new level, barrels flying everywhere. I remember the depths of that adrenaline rush. Which tech trend do you wish would go away?People walking their dogs while looking at their phones. Maddening. What is one thing you wish you had created?The soundtrack to Birth. Alexandre Desplat is a shape-shifting genius, and that movie, in addition to being peculiar and amazing, has one of the most haunting scores ever. What creation are you most proud of?My children. I was, of course, only a co-creator. My partner Karla might say no, more of a production assistant. Which are you least proud of?I feel fondly towards a lot of my early music and stories. They were what I needed to make at the time. But I’m glad the bulk happened before the internet became the permanent archive, so they’ve been conveniently lost to time. What’s the best piece of advice you’ve ever received?Richard Nodell, my longtime work coach slash therapist slash pal, once told me: “Your job is not to know where you’re going but to lead the search” ... wise words. What is your current obsession?The Finnish composer Kaija Saariaho (who somehow made the orchestral sound like storms passing over fields) and UK dubstep, the dark underground grimy amalgam of garage, drum n bass, 2-step and Jamaican dub that preceded the American variant sometimes not-wrongly called “bro-step.” What do you do when you need to focus?I meditate for two minutes and then brick my phone. What do you do when you’re feeling stuck?I set up some gamelan strips on a table in my room, and I bang on those when I need a reset. When was the last time you went somewhere without your phone?I go places without my phone all the time. But then again, I have an Apple Watch, so it probably doesn’t count. What’s the last piece of physical media you bought?My son and I bought an original pressing of John Coltrane’s Blue Train about a month ago. What do you think is worth splurging on?Food and music. What would the tagline for your biopic be? “In a world of noise, one man heard the signal.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. SpaceX’s ambitious, expensive gamble to replace AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon
2026-10-17

SpaceX’s ambitious, expensive gamble to replace AT&T, T-Mobile, and Verizon

The Verge | 2026-10-17

Visit Original Website
Cell service isn’t rocket ships or flying cars, but it could be a booming business for SpaceX. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. Today, I’m talking with Walter Piecyk, a partner and analyst at LightShed Partners who covers tech, media, and telecom, about SpaceX’s recently announced plan to become a major wireless carrier. Last week, SpaceX agreed to buy a big chunk of low-band spectrum to expand the Starlink network, spectrum that’s been traded around the telecom business for decades now. SpaceX’s ambitious plan is to use that spectrum to help build “the world’s most advanced mobile network.” Verge subscribers, don’t forget you get exclusive access to ad-free Decoder wherever you get your podcasts. Head here. Not a subscriber? You can sign up here. Combined with news that the FCC approved the deployment of 15,000 next-gen Starlink satellites and the company’s previous agreement to purchase close to $20 billion worth of EchoStar spectrum last year, it’s now clear SpaceX is serious about competing with AT&T, Verizon, and T-Mobile. I’ve covered the telecom business for a long time, and I’ve always known that these companies can say they want to do something until they run into the harsh reality of how expensive it is to actually do it. The telecom graveyard of pivots, bad ideas, and failed acquisitions is vast. Walt has been closely monitoring SpaceX’s acquisition, and he’s correctly predicted that the company was headed in this direction for months now. So I wanted to dig into whether SpaceX can pull this off, why Walt thinks the existing telecom giants have really fumbled their response to SpaceX’s growing threat, and what happens when Elon Musk’s rocket company jumps headfirst into a business everyone loves to hate. Okay: LightShed Partners’ Walter Piecyk on SpaceX’s plan to expand into mobile. Here we go. This interview has been lightly edited for length and clarity. Walter Piecyk, you’re a partner and a tech, media, and telecom analyst at LightShed Partners. Welcome to Decoder. Thanks for having me. I’m looking forward to it. I’m excited to talk to you. I feel like we’ve been tweeting or posting around each other for many, many years, but I’m a telecom nerd at heart. I’ve been covering the space for ages. SpaceX is going to make a big splash. It’s going to be the fourth major mobile carrier in the United States and maybe the world. It bought some spectrum to do it. It’s EchoStar spectrum by way of private equity. It is at once very simple and also very complicated. Give us the state of the state. SpaceX bought 800 Mhz spectrum for $8 billion. They spent nearly $20 billion already on separate EchoStar spectrum. What is SpaceX trying to do here? It’s definitely complicated, but maybe less complicated than trying to figure out how to put a data center out in space and making that work. Or something on the moon. But they don’t typically want to own spectrum and spend for spectrum. If you think about how Starlink was built, satellite companies don’t typically pay for spectrum. And then as the business evolved and as they saw the opportunity in telecom, they purchased some spectrum initially from EchoStar. The feedback they got from investors at that time was very positive and they said, “Wait a minute, you should do more here.” This is a trillion-dollar enterprise value business in the US alone. Obviously they have global aspirations both for Starlink as well as what they’re doing with this mobile telephone service. So they bought more. They went back to EchoStar. That’s a key thing to pay attention to, in that they bought some spectrum and then they went back and bought some more. Because with a lot of things that Elon Musk does, he learns as he goes. He bought the spectrum, got feedback, talked to people in telecom, and bought some more spectrum. Then we didn’t hear from them for a while. Now, you have this thing that happened last week where they bought some more spectrum, and I can go through the history of it if you care, but it’s long, wonky telecom stuff. This was originally the Nextel spectrum, if you remember that company. I do. Actually, I do want you to go through the history because I think the telecom business is best understood as a bunch of lobbyists trying to make everything more complicated than it should be. Yes, spectrum auctions are complicated and what spectrum is good for what is deeply tied to the physics of the radio spectrum and all that. But here it’s the 800 MHz spectrum from the Nextel service, the old push-to-talk, walkie-talkie service that Sprint had to divest as part of the deal to get sold to T-Mobile. They’ve been trying to sell it for ages. They sold it to this company, Grain Management. They’ve been sitting on it, and when Starlink showed up, Elon bought it. Nobody else wanted it. What did he see in the spectrum that no one else did? When you realize that you need to build a terrestrial network to support what you’re doing with satellites, because satellites work fine if you’ve got your dish on the top of your house and you’re getting your broadband, but when you’re trying to connect with a phone that doesn’t have the same type of power or you’re going inside a home, you’re going to need better coverage. Sirius Satellite also has supplementary towers. There are not that many of them, but sometimes when you get out of Sirius coverage, you will connect to a tower. When they bought that EchoStar spectrum and then got FCC approval, and if you looked into those details of that FCC approval and what was required, you would see that they can’t do this by satellites alone. There had to be terrestrial. Then SpaceX, as Elon is wont to do, tries to send some diversions like, “We’re going to do Femtocell, we’re not going to do macrocell, this, that, and the other thing.” But they realized that they were going to build a terrestrial network. Now they finally realized, “If I’m going to offer mobile service, I need some form of a terrestrial network.” At the same time, the telcos are not going to help them out. They’re not going to give them a wholesale agreement. Then when they go back and say the companies that had the lowest churn, what is the fundamental value you need in a terrestrial network? It’s low-band spectrum. Why? It travels further, you can get better coverage, and you have that connectivity. So getting this low-band spectrum piece was just an important part, not to make the satellites work better, but to actually give them the spectrum assets that they need to build a terrestrial network. That’s why the stocks reacted, because people finally came to terms with the fact that Elon is actually going to build a terrestrial network. I’m less interested in the stock and more interested in just the cost structure of how this works. If I rewind my brain to being a baby blogger at Engadget 100 years ago, the FCC told all the TV networks they had to stop broadcasting on 700 MHz and we were going to run an auction. Verizon and AT&T bought big chunks of 700 MHz spectrum and that’s what 4G is built on. That’s the low-bandwidth spectrum that you’re talking about. It goes everywhere. This was a big victory that we reallocated 700 MHz, and 800 MHz was sitting right there the whole time. This is the Nextel spectrum. If it is so valuable, why wasn’t there a market for it until Elon showed up just now? It was part of this transaction. T-Mobile ended up having to sell it because the government said, “If we’re going to allow you to take out the fourth competitor, which was Sprint, then in order to enable another fourth competitor out there, we need to have you guys give up some of your low-band spectrum.” Part of the deal that T-Mobile and Sprint had with the government was that they would sell it to EchoStar. Why? Because Charlie Ergen, CEO of Dish at the time — it’s EchoStar now — was going to be the fourth competitor. This was the rationale that they gave in terms of the approval of the transaction. Dish needed low-band spectrum. The problem is Ergen went about his business, he was trying to build a network, and then he just couldn’t afford to buy that. They had the rights to buy. Then at that point when Ergen failed on the bid to come up with the dollars to buy that spectrum, it enabled T-Mobile and actually forced them to put it up for auction. Now, your question: Why didn’t someone else show up then? Again, from SpaceX’s standpoint, as I mentioned at the start of this podcast, their strategy has been evolving over time. In terms of the other telcos, they can’t really use it. There are some limitations that they have. But in hindsight, maybe Verizon or AT&T should have bought this 800 MHz spectrum for whatever it was, let’s call it $8 billion. They’re probably losing more in enterprise value over the concerns of what’s happening with SpaceX than what it cost them just to buy it in what’s known as foreclose. They basically just take it, even if they don’t use it or do whatever they need to meet those build out requirements and stop SpaceX from having unnecessary assets. I don’t think even these companies were taking Elon seriously and thinking that he would actually build the network. Do you think that’s colored by the failure of Dish? We covered the promises of Dish Network to build a fourth wireless carrier in this country so closely. Their attempt was predicated on some amount of technology innovation. They were going to build this thing called an O-RAN network, which had a more competitive supplier base instead of just Nokia or Siemens or whoever, and they were going to run it on AWS and that was going to reduce costs. Man, eventually they supported one Motorola handset in Vegas and even that was backstopped by AT&T. They literally could not do it. These promises never came true. Dish is a different company and Charlie Ergen’s a different CEO and their ability to just throw money at a problem until it’s solved is very different from Elon. But if they couldn’t innovate to make a cheaper, faster network, what’s the evidence that Starlink can stand up an entire terrestrial network at a cost basis that allows them to compete selling service to consumers, while they’re also standing up an entire network in space? Because that feels like adding up a lot of cost. I’m going to go back even deeper than that. Remember when Sprint was struggling, Masayoshi Son from SoftBank was claiming the same thing. They were going to cut their CapEx to nothing and they were going to use these small cells. They innovated how wireless was going to get delivered. That failed. Obviously, they had to get bailed out through the purchase when T-Mobile bought Sprint effectively for just the spectrum. That network got completely decommissioned. To your point, Dish got a network built, but they couldn’t resonate in the market. The difference between Dish and SpaceX is Elon’s ability to get customers. That’s where Dish truly failed. They built the network. The network worked fine. They had the wholesale agreements that they needed. I’ll push back on you a little bit there. I have a very funny email from a Dish PR person from several years ago where they said, “We’d like to issue a correction to your story. Our network does exist.” That was the level that they were competing at, where they had to insist to us that the network was real, even though we could not find it in our tests in the various cities they claim. Maybe there was a network, they built some amount of network, but they had not built anything that competed at the national scale with the big three. Absolutely. It’s not like your phone. The phone that I was using, I had an iPhone that it was mostly going to roam onto the AT&T and T-Mobile wholesale agreements that existed. The theory is you get the customers, you have a go-to-market strategy, and then you backfill the network. This is probably going to be something similar. You can even go back to when John Legere first took over T-Mobile. They had a crappy network, and then Legere promised, “We’re going to build it,” taking on customers saying that the network was great before the network was great. Then it caught up because they actually finally got the Germans, Deutsche Telekom, to give them the money to invest in the network. The same thing can happen here where SpaceX will sell something that’ll probably rely on other assets that are out there, even if the telcos themselves are circling the wagons. They have the satellites for certain benefits in terms of coverage and then they can build the capacity over time. The point is it’s going to take time. You can’t just turn on a network overnight and start selling service on that. This has always been my framework of how to think about telecom companies. You can try to run the network cheaper or you can run some kind of different technology to make that cheaper. Or you can just spend and have the best network, which was the Verizon strategy for years and years and years. Then you’ve got to acquire and manage your customers. That was the T-Mobile strategy. That was John Legere. He was just nicer to people than AT&T was, and he picked up a lot of customers and that drove some investment. Then you’ve got to get some devices. You have to have the iPhone. We can set that aside because it feels like that era of competition is over. We’re not doing exclusive devices anymore. But those are the two. I’m just looking at the network here. It does not seem to me, from the outside, that Starlink is proposing to build a cheaper network. It feels like they’re proposing to build a vastly more expensive network because they need the satellites. They’re going to launch 15,000 more satellites to support this network. Then they’re going to build a terrestrial network by investing in spectrum on the ground and building some set of base stations. Then they’re going to have to convince one of the big three to let them roam over as some sort of mobile virtual network operator (MVNO). The big three do not want to do that. So they’re going to overcharge them for that. I’m just stuck at that being a lot of cost, even if you’re Elon Musk and you can make some set of people buy whatever you’re selling. The third thing you said is obviously the most challenging. That’s the tent and the pole that prevents you from launching services sooner rather than later. But without necessarily getting a wholesale agreement, there might be other ways. Roaming agreements exist out there. There are roaming partners that exist that these telcos still rely on. It’s very expensive. It’s not something you can build a high-margin business on, but it perhaps provides an entry to the market sooner rather than later. As Apple has pitched in terms of satellite connectivity, some people are in the middle of the Utah desert texting because they’re injured. There’s value there. But it’s the soccer field. We have, maybe not in the cities, but in the suburbs, plenty of areas that the telcos have effectively given up on trying to provide coverage because of NIMBY-ism, meaning people just don’t want these cell sites. This satellite coverage can give you not just this basic text messaging that Apple has been doing through the Globalstar constellation, but a true cellphone-type experience. So that’s a positive. The second differentiation is the fact that these telcos have 60 percent earnings before interest, taxes, depreciation, and amortization (EBITDA) margins. So there’s a lot of fat there that can be eaten off of, that when you’re selling other services in terms of Elon, like home broadband, maybe this becomes an entree in terms of pushing Grok and people’s usage. You don’t necessarily need 60 percent margins, in this case, for him to justify building this network. They also talk about other data connectivity that these satellites are going to enable. Again, in the current constellation, you need a lot of power and you need a dish. But if you don’t have that, if you have the cell phone-type power, then the internet of things (IOT) and other types of opportunities that exist there become another revenue opportunity that they look at. It’s just funny. The IOT story — that’s what all these companies promised us about 4G. That’s what they all promised us about 5G. There’s something about this business that just rhymes every time. They’re going to do self-driving cars and robot surgery. I’m confident we’re going to hear about self-driving cars and robot surgery when it comes to Starlink and Tesla and Optimus. There’s something about these applications for next generation networks that just recurs, and we hear about them all the time. Then I think about our consumer audience at The Verge, and they want long battery life and fast service and [connectivity] all the places they go. I’m not sure how that squares with your phone is going to connect directly to a satellite in the middle of a soccer field. There’s something about that that also seems challenging. It’s just the performance if your phone does connect to the satellite on the soccer field and it works. A lot of the things that people said wouldn’t work on Starlink in terms of airplanes or even getting customer-premises equipment (CPE) down below a certain price, they’ve delivered on. Obviously, Elon sometimes delivers on them a little later than some of his initial optimistic promises, but they ultimately get delivered on. There is the ability with these new constellations to give you that type of connectivity on these spaces. Is that enough to differentiate with consumers? I don’t know. The telcos have attacked this by saying, “No one cares about our direct-to-device (D2D) services.” Well, your D2D services are not good. It’s just sending basic text messages. What they’re talking about is giving you that blanket coverage where you get what you expect from your cell phone wherever you go. That’s fine, but you still have to give it to the person in the middle of the building where their cell phone works today and provide the volume at a cost that is going to be attractive to that consumer. So those are longer-term things that are going to require some amount of terrestrial network build. Then the question is: how much? A lot of the attacks that have been out there have been referencing $100 billion, $200 billion. I get that. Why? Telcos each spend $10 billion to $15 billion a year, but they are maintaining massive customer bases. To get that initial network up where you have that usage, where you use that low-band spectrum to get into buildings, is not those types of numbers. You’re probably talking like $10 billion, $15 billion for that initial build in terms of CapEx. The dynamic there is really interesting. I’m just thinking about AT&T when the iPhone came out. The network immediately got overloaded because there was a surge of customer demand that took four years to truly resolve. Part of that resolution was just putting the iPhone on the other carriers. How do you think that will play out for Starlink here? Because right now, Starlink customer service is not the best in the industry. Their actual service, people love it. And then when they need customer service, it’s a bunch of automated systems and people complaining on Reddit. So there’s some challenge there if you’re running a consumer business at this scale. For consumer businesses, think about all the advertising that’s done. The fulfillment, dealing with the phones, phone subsidies, and that’s a big part of the financing. How many customers are we talking about? So for the amount of spectrum that they have now in the network that I’m talking about for $10–$15 billion, you’re not taking 50 percent share overnight. I don’t think that would be that expectation from day one. But then the theory of telco is that when you do that initial CapEx build, you have your initial spectrum purchases — $10, $20, maybe $30 billion — another $10 or 15 billion in terms of CapEx. If you are successful and you do get that five or 10 million customers, the opportunity to invest more happens. If you go back to the old days when the cable view of wireless was going to be, “We’re going to get these MVNOs. We’re going to drive the wireless companies into the dirt and then we’re just going to buy them.” That happened in at least one European market, and that was the cable bull thesis that was occurring. You could have the same bull thesis emerge from the SpaceX bulls here where they’re going to say, “We’ll get those first five or 10 million customers. We’ll drive them into the ground. Verizon’s going to be so desperate for any type of revenue because their revenue declined. They’re just going to hand them a wholesale deal and then they’ll start selling spectrum. There’s going to be a spectrum auction with the FCC.” You can see how the narrative can build based on, “An initial CapEx that’s not that big, get in the market, and then if it’s successful, we start investing more.” Let’s talk about the terrestrial network for one more turn. I heard you throw a little water on the idea that they’re going to build femtocells. That is what Elon and Gwynne Shotwell have been saying — that they don’t need to build the traditional kind of terrestrial network that AT&T and Verizon and T-Mobile have, where you have a lot of fiber back haul, the big sites that require all kinds of zoning permits and everything. That they can basically just put small cell stations next to Starlink dishes, put them anywhere they want, and blanket the world with them. And Starlink itself will be the backhaul. I sense some problems with this. Starlink is not 100 percent reliable all the time. If you have a network that is not 100 percent reliable all the time, you’ve got some problems, so maybe you have to backstop it somehow. But you don’t think that’s what they’re doing. You think they’re going to build a regular terrestrial network. I’m sure that’ll be a part of it, but you need macro towers for that type of coverage and penetration and power. Not to explain physics to SpaceX, they probably know it better than anybody, but what you’ll see is as they talk more about this low-band spectrum that they purchase, when we get on the conference calls of these tower companies, you’re going to find out that you’re going to see a lot of those antennas show up on towers. It’s just natural. You can talk about femtocells, but it’s hard to see that the network’s going to get built without macro towers. One of the weirder aspects of the telecom industry, as it’s constituted in the United States right now, is that cell carriers own some of their towers and operate some of their towers. And then there are these crazy third parties that literally have names like TowerCo that operate a bunch of the other towers. Are those captured? Do you think SpaceX can get deals with those companies, or are they beholden to their big partners? These are huge real estate investment trusts (REITs). American Tower is the biggest one. It’s probably one of the largest companies in this. When you consider any REITs, there are other companies out there. They have these towers. They have space in many cases where basically SpaceX can just go and get an additional antenna. It’s a lot easier to do that now than when the wireless companies were initially building this stuff and having to erect their own towers. In addition, EchoStar is going through a bankruptcy. They have a lot of radios that could be used, that could be bought on the cheap, that could expedite SpaceX’s entrance in the market. They haven’t shown interest in that yet. The EchoStar bankruptcy process hasn’t yet concluded, but EchoStar can still be a potential partner here by selling them radios that are already on towers. You’ve lifted the amount of time. Those radios use the spectrum that SpaceX is purchasing, so it can be a quick fix for them to enter the market. Who knows if they do that? EchoStar has some remaining spectrum that Verizon really needs. At this point, there is some strategic sense to just buy EchoStar outright — because EchoStar is getting stock that was issued to them at a discount. If you’re buying them, you’re effectively buying in your own stock. You don’t have to issue the cash to the company and you’re getting whatever cash proceeds that EchoStar is getting from some of its spectrum sales. This is a bigger and more obvious issue for Apple than anybody else, but it does change how the telcos are going to act and the role that they’re going to play and where that margin should be. They gave up a lot of value to Apple at some point during this continuum of the wireless industry. We’re going through another paradigm change, so we’ll see how those value splits play out. Walter, we’re going to have to have you back soon because it feels like these things are going to happen fast. I was very happy to nerd out about telecom with you. Thanks for being on Decoder. This implant can restore sight for patients with macular degeneration
2026-10-16

This implant can restore sight for patients with macular degeneration

MIT Technology Review | 2026-10-16

Visit Original Website
More than 5 million people around the world with age-related macular degeneration (AMD) experience atrophy in their retinas. And until now, no treatment could restore their vision. The PRIMA system, which was approved in Europe in July and is awaiting FDA clearance in the US, changes that. It combines a retinal implant with camera-­equipped eyeglasses to improve sight in patients who develop a blind spot in the middle of their vision due to advanced AMD. After training sessions, more than 80% of recipients were able to read, do crosswords, and use their phones. Developed by the California-based Science Corporation, the system takes advantage of the fact that AMD doesn’t damage all retinal circuitry; only photoreceptors—the cells that convert light into an electrical signal—die off. PRIMA’s implant mimics those cells and transmits visual information directly to healthy eye circuitry and on to the brain. “We’re leveraging all of the body’s own natural functions to process and transmit the information,” says Frank Brodie, an ophthalmologist at the University of California, San Francisco, and Science Corp.'s chief medical officer for ophthalmology. Glasses outfitted with a miniature front-facing camera capture what someone is seeing in real time. The video feed is roughly five megapixels running at 30 frames per second, which is similar to TV. The raw video is transmitted to a connected processor via a fiber-optic cable. A small, handheld processor, which can be set on a table or tucked in a pocket, converts the video feed into infrared light signals that route back to the glasses. The device also includes physical buttons that let the wearer adjust contrast, brightness, and zoom. A laser projector on the glasses faces the wearer and shines pulses of near-infrared light directly at the eye at 880 nanometers—an invisible wavelength. This signal passes through the pupil and on to the retinal implant in the back of the eye. The implant is made up of 378 pixels, each with an electrode and two photovoltaic cells, which do double duty. They convert the light into a current that healthy bipolar and ganglion cells pass on to the brain. They also act as solar cells to power the chip, eliminating the need for a battery. Update: This story has been updated to clarify the specific cases of AMD the PRIMA system treats. Linda Marsa is a former Los Angeles Times reporter and an author who covers medicine, health, science, and the environment. Scientists hope it could be used to reconstruct a person’s inner thoughts, mental images, or even dreams. Could the drugs affect hair, nails, and vision? Generation Lab claims its drug combo can “stop the spread of aging” around the body. And it’s looking for influencers to give it a try. New studies pour cold water on a prominent rejuvenation idea.
